\section{Cross product and rigid-body
velocity}\label{cross-product-and-rigid-body-velocity}

Let \(\mathbf u\) and \(\mathbf v\) be geometric vectors with coordinate
columns \({}^{a}\mathbf u,{}^{a}\mathbf v\in\mathbb R^3\) expressed in
the same right-handed orthonormal frame \(\{a\}\). Their cross product,
expressed in frame \(\{a\}\), is defined by

\[
{}^{a}\mathbf{u}\times{}^{a}\mathbf{v}
=
\begin{bmatrix}
u_yv_z-u_zv_y \\
u_zv_x-u_xv_z \\
u_xv_y-u_yv_x
\end{bmatrix}
\in\mathbb R^3.
\]

Its direction follows the right-hand rule. If both vectors are nonzero,
let \(\theta\in[0,\pi]\) be the smaller angle between them. The
cross-product magnitude is

\[
\left\|{}^{a}\mathbf{u}\times{}^{a}\mathbf{v}\right\|_2
=
\|\mathbf{u}\|_2\|\mathbf{v}\|_2\sin\theta.
\]

The cross product is perpendicular to both input vectors because

\[
{}^{a}\mathbf{u}^\mathsf{T}
\left({}^{a}\mathbf{u}\times{}^{a}\mathbf{v}\right)=0,
\qquad
{}^{a}\mathbf{v}^\mathsf{T}
\left({}^{a}\mathbf{u}\times{}^{a}\mathbf{v}\right)=0.
\]

\subsection{Example: velocity of a point on a rotating rigid
body}\label{example-velocity-of-a-point-on-a-rotating-rigid-body}

Let \(O\) and \(P\) be two points fixed to the same rigid body. Point
\(O\) is the chosen reference point; it need not be stationary, and it
need not be the body's centre. The vector \({}^{a}\mathbf{r}_{OP}\) runs
from \(O\) to \(P\). Although this vector is fixed relative to the body,
its coordinates generally change as the body rotates. Express every
quantity in the same orthonormal frame \(\{a\}\):

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \({}^{a}\boldsymbol{\omega}\) as the body's angular-velocity vector,
  measured in \(\mathrm{rad\,s^{-1}}\);
\item
  \({}^{a}\mathbf{r}_{OP}\) as the position of \(P\) relative to \(O\),
  measured in \(\mathrm{m}\);
\item
  \({}^{a}\mathbf{v}_O\) as the linear velocity of \(O\), measured in
  \(\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\); and
\item
  \({}^{a}\mathbf{v}_P\) as the linear velocity of \(P\), measured in
  \(\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\).
\end{itemize}

At any instant, the motion of \(P\) can be understood as the sum of two
simultaneous contributions:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Translation with \(O\).} If the body were not rotating, \(P\)
  would share the reference-point velocity \({}^{a}\mathbf v_O\).
\item
  \textbf{Motion relative to \(O\) caused by rotation.} Rotation adds a
  velocity whose direction and magnitude depend on
  \({}^{a}\boldsymbol{\omega}\) and \({}^{a}\mathbf r_{OP}\).
\end{enumerate}

The angular-velocity vector points along the instantaneous rotation axis
according to the right-hand rule, and its magnitude is the angular
speed. Define the rotational contribution to the velocity of \(P\) by

\[
{}^{a}\mathbf{v}_{P/O}
\mathrel{:=}
{}^{a}\boldsymbol{\omega}\times{}^{a}\mathbf{r}_{OP}.
\]

The cross product determines both the direction and the magnitude of
this contribution. In particular,

\[
{}^{a}\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\mathsf T}{}^{a}\mathbf{v}_{P/O}=0,
\qquad
{}^{a}\mathbf{r}_{OP}^{\mathsf T}{}^{a}\mathbf{v}_{P/O}=0.
\]

Therefore, \({}^{a}\mathbf{v}_{P/O}\) is perpendicular to the rotation
axis and to \({}^{a}\mathbf r_{OP}\). It points tangent to the
instantaneous circular path generated by the rotation. Adding the
translational and rotational contributions gives the rigid-body velocity
relation

\[
\boxed{
{}^{a}\mathbf{v}_P
=
{}^{a}\mathbf{v}_O
+{}^{a}\boldsymbol{\omega}\times{}^{a}\mathbf{r}_{OP}
}.
\]

\subsubsection{Velocity-vector
construction}\label{velocity-vector-construction}

The construction in Figure~\ref{fig-rigid-body-point-velocity} shows
this addition for one instant:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  The green arrow \({}^{a}\mathbf r_{OP}\) locates \(P\) relative to
  \(O\).
\item
  The magenta arrow is the reference-point velocity
  \({}^{a}\mathbf v_O\). It is deliberately not parallel to
  \({}^{a}\mathbf r_{OP}\).
\item
  The orange arrow is the rotational contribution
  \({}^{a}\boldsymbol{\omega}\times{}^{a}\mathbf r_{OP}={}^{a}\mathbf v_{P/O}\).
  The right-angle marker shows that it is perpendicular to
  \({}^{a}\mathbf r_{OP}\).
\item
  The dashed arrows are translated copies of the two velocity
  contributions. Moving a vector without rotating or resizing it does
  not change the vector, so the copies can be arranged head to tail.
\item
  The blue arrow joins the beginning of the first contribution to the
  end of the second. It is therefore the resultant velocity
  \({}^{a}\mathbf v_P\).
\end{itemize}

In this planar view, \({}^{a}\boldsymbol{\omega}\) points along
\(+z_a\), out of the page. The right-hand rule then gives the displayed
tangential direction for
\({}^{a}\boldsymbol{\omega}\times{}^{a}\mathbf r_{OP}\). Notice that
only the rotational contribution must be perpendicular to
\({}^{a}\mathbf r_{OP}\). The total velocity \({}^{a}\mathbf v_P\)
generally is not, because \({}^{a}\mathbf v_O\) may point in any
direction.

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\includegraphics[width=1\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{index_files/mediabag/figures/rigid_body_point_velocity.pdf}

}

\caption{\label{fig-rigid-body-point-velocity}Velocity construction for
two points on a rigid body. The rotational contribution is perpendicular
to the position vector, while the reference-point velocity is
independent of that perpendicular relationship.}

\end{figure}%

For pure rotation about a fixed axis through \(O\), the reference point
is stationary, so \({}^{a}\mathbf{v}_O=\mathbf{0}\). Therefore,

\[
\boxed{{}^{a}\mathbf{v}_P
=
{}^{a}\boldsymbol{\omega}\times{}^{a}\mathbf{r}_{OP}}.
\]

If \(\phi\) is the angle between \({}^{a}\boldsymbol{\omega}\) and
\({}^{a}\mathbf{r}_{OP}\), then the relative speed caused by rotation is

\[
\left\|{}^{a}\mathbf{v}_{P/O}\right\|_2
=
\left\|{}^{a}\boldsymbol{\omega}\right\|_2
\left\|{}^{a}\mathbf{r}_{OP}\right\|_2
\sin\phi
=
\omega r_\perp,
\]

where \(r_\perp\) is the perpendicular distance from \(P\) to the
rotation axis. A point on the axis has \(r_\perp=0\) and therefore has
no relative linear velocity due to rotation. Under the pure-rotation
assumption, \({}^{a}\mathbf{v}_P={}^{a}\mathbf{v}_{P/O}\).

For planar counter-clockwise rotation about the positive \(z\)-axis,

\[
{}^{a}\boldsymbol{\omega}
=
\begin{bmatrix}
0\\0\\\omega
\end{bmatrix},
\qquad
{}^{a}\mathbf{r}_{OP}
=
\begin{bmatrix}
x\\y\\0
\end{bmatrix}.
\]

The point velocity is

\[
{}^{a}\mathbf{v}_P
=
{}^{a}\boldsymbol{\omega}\times{}^{a}\mathbf{r}_{OP}
=
\begin{bmatrix}
-\omega y\\
\omega x\\
0
\end{bmatrix}.
\]

For example, if \(\omega=2\,\mathrm{rad\,s^{-1}}\) and
\({}^{a}\mathbf{r}_{OP}=[0.5\;\;0\;\;0]^\mathsf{T}\,\mathrm{m}\), then

\[
{}^{a}\mathbf{v}_P
=
\begin{bmatrix}
0\\1\\0
\end{bmatrix}
\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}.
\]

The point lies on the positive \(x\)-axis, while its instantaneous
velocity points along the positive \(y\)-axis, tangent to its circular
path.

\section*{Quick test A: cross products and rigid-body
velocity}\label{quick-test-a-cross-products-and-rigid-body-velocity}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quick test A: cross products and
rigid-body velocity}

\markright{Quick test A: cross products and rigid-body velocity}

Complete this test without looking back at the section.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Recall:} What rule determines the direction of
  \(\mathbf u\times\mathbf v\)?
\item
  \textbf{Explanation:} Why is \(\mathbf u\times\mathbf v\)
  perpendicular to both input vectors?
\item
  \textbf{Derivation:} Substitute the component formula for
  \({}^{a}\mathbf u\times{}^{a}\mathbf v\) into
  \({}^{a}\mathbf u^{\mathsf T}({}^{a}\mathbf u\times{}^{a}\mathbf v)\)
  and show that the terms cancel.
\item
  \textbf{Application:} Compute
  \([1\;\;2\;\;0]^\mathsf T\times[0\;\;1\;\;0]^\mathsf T\).
\item
  \textbf{Limitations:} What happens to the cross product when the input
  vectors are parallel, and why does that prevent it from defining a
  unique normal direction?
\item
  \textbf{Transfer:} At one instant, a rigid body has
  \({}^{a}\boldsymbol{\omega}=[0\;\;0\;\;2]^\mathsf{T}\,\mathrm{rad\,s^{-1}}\),
  \({}^{a}\mathbf r_{OP}=[1\;\;0\;\;0]^\mathsf{T}\,\mathrm{m}\), and
  \({}^{a}\mathbf v_O=[0.5\;\;0.5\;\;0]^\mathsf{T}\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\).
  Find \({}^{a}\mathbf v_{P/O}\) and \({}^{a}\mathbf v_P\). Which
  velocity is perpendicular to \({}^{a}\mathbf r_{OP}\)?
\end{enumerate}

\subsection*{Answers and hints}\label{answers-and-hints-4}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Answers and hints}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  The right-hand rule, applied in the order from \(\mathbf u\) to
  \(\mathbf v\).
\item
  Its dot product with either input is zero.
\item
  Expanding gives
  \(u_x(u_yv_z-u_zv_y)+u_y(u_zv_x-u_xv_z)+u_z(u_xv_y-u_yv_x)=0\) after
  pairwise cancellation.
\item
  The result is \([0\;\;0\;\;1]^\mathsf T\).
\item
  Parallel vectors have \(\sin\theta=0\), so their cross product is the
  zero vector. A zero vector has no direction and therefore cannot
  specify a unique normal.
\item
  \({}^{a}\mathbf v_{P/O}=[0\;\;2\;\;0]^\mathsf T\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\)
  and
  \({}^{a}\mathbf v_P=[0.5\;\;2.5\;\;0]^\mathsf T\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\).
  The rotational contribution is perpendicular to
  \({}^{a}\mathbf r_{OP}\); the total velocity is not.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Coordinate frames}\label{coordinate-frames}

\subsection{What defines a frame?}\label{what-defines-a-frame}

A three-dimensional coordinate frame \(\{a\}\) consists of:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  an origin \(O_a\), which is the physical point from which positions in
  frame \(\{a\}\) are measured; and
\item
  three right-handed orthonormal basis vectors \(\mathbf e_{x,a}\),
  \(\mathbf e_{y,a}\), and \(\mathbf e_{z,a}\), which define the
  positive \(x_a\)-, \(y_a\)-, and \(z_a\)-axis directions.
\end{enumerate}

The subscript \(a\) associates the origin and basis vectors with frame
\(\{a\}\). Orthonormal means that each basis vector has unit length and
that distinct basis vectors are perpendicular. Right-handed means that
\(\mathbf e_{x,a}\times\mathbf e_{y,a}=\mathbf e_{z,a}\).

A free vector depends only on the basis orientation used to describe it.
A point also depends on the frame origin. This distinction explains why
changing the coordinate frame of a free vector requires only a rotation,
whereas changing the coordinates of a point generally requires a
rotation and a translation.

\subsection{Orientation convention}\label{orientation-convention}

Let \(\{a\}\) and \(\{b\}\) be three-dimensional right-handed
orthonormal frames. Define \(\mathbf R_{ab}\in\mathbb R^{3\times3}\) as
the rotation matrix that maps coordinate columns from frame \(\{b\}\) to
frame \(\{a\}\). Its columns are the basis vectors of \(\{b\}\)
expressed in frame \(\{a\}\):

\[
\mathbf{R}_{ab}
=
\begin{bmatrix}
{}^{a}\mathbf{e}_{x,b} &
{}^{a}\mathbf{e}_{y,b} &
{}^{a}\mathbf{e}_{z,b}
\end{bmatrix},
\]

where each column \({}^{a}\mathbf e_{i,b}\in\mathbb R^3\) is an axis of
frame \(\{b\}\) written using the basis of frame \(\{a\}\), for
\(i\in\{x,y,z\}\).

The following notation distinguishes a geometric vector from its
coordinate representations:

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Symbol
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Meaning
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\(\mathbf v\) & A geometric vector, independent of the coordinate frame
used to describe it. \\
\(\mathbf e_{x,b}\) & The geometric unit vector along the positive
\(x_b\)-axis of frame \(\{b\}\). \\
\([\mathbf v]_b={}^{b}\mathbf v\in\mathbb R^3\) & The numerical
coordinate column of \(\mathbf v\) in frame \(\{b\}\). \\
\([\mathbf v]_a={}^{a}\mathbf v\in\mathbb R^3\) & The numerical
coordinate column of \(\mathbf v\) in frame \(\{a\}\). \\
\([\mathbf e_{x,b}]_a={}^{a}\mathbf e_{x,b}\in\mathbb R^3\) & The
coordinate column of frame \(\{b\}\)'s geometric \(x\)-axis, expressed
in frame \(\{a\}\). \\
\end{longtable}
}

The bracket operator \([\,\cdot\,]_a\) means ``take the coordinate
column of the enclosed geometric vector in frame \(\{a\}\).'' The
geometric vector \(\mathbf v\) can be expanded using either frame's
basis:

\[
\boxed{
\mathbf v
=
v_x^{(b)}\mathbf e_{x,b}
+
v_y^{(b)}\mathbf e_{y,b}
+
v_z^{(b)}\mathbf e_{z,b}
=
v_x^{(a)}\mathbf e_{x,a}
+
v_y^{(a)}\mathbf e_{y,a}
+
v_z^{(a)}\mathbf e_{z,a}
}.
\]

Here, \(v_i^{(b)}\in\mathbb R\) and \(v_i^{(a)}\in\mathbb R\) are the
scalar components of \(\mathbf v\) along axis \(i\in\{x,y,z\}\) of
frames \(\{b\}\) and \(\{a\}\), respectively. The corresponding
coordinate columns are

\[
{}^{b}\mathbf v
=
[\mathbf v]_b
=
\begin{bmatrix}
v_x^{(b)}\\
v_y^{(b)}\\
v_z^{(b)}
\end{bmatrix},
\qquad
{}^{a}\mathbf v
=
[\mathbf v]_a
=
\begin{bmatrix}
v_x^{(a)}\\
v_y^{(a)}\\
v_z^{(a)}
\end{bmatrix}.
\]

Apply the coordinate operator \([\,\cdot\,]_a\) to the expansion of
\(\mathbf v\) in the frame-\(\{b\}\) basis. Because taking coordinates
is a linear operation,

\[
[\mathbf v]_a
=
v_x^{(b)}[\mathbf e_{x,b}]_a
+
v_y^{(b)}[\mathbf e_{y,b}]_a
+
v_z^{(b)}[\mathbf e_{z,b}]_a.
\]

Using the equivalent superscript notation gives

\[
{}^{a}\mathbf v
=
v_x^{(b)}\,{}^{a}\mathbf e_{x,b}
+
v_y^{(b)}\,{}^{a}\mathbf e_{y,b}
+
v_z^{(b)}\,{}^{a}\mathbf e_{z,b}.
\]

Writing this linear combination as matrix-vector multiplication gives

\[
{}^{a}\mathbf v
=
\underbrace{
\begin{bmatrix}
{}^{a}\mathbf e_{x,b} &
{}^{a}\mathbf e_{y,b} &
{}^{a}\mathbf e_{z,b}
\end{bmatrix}
}_{\mathbf R_{ab}}
\underbrace{
\begin{bmatrix}
v_x^{(b)}\\
v_y^{(b)}\\
v_z^{(b)}
\end{bmatrix}
}_{{}^{b}\mathbf v}.
\]

Therefore,

\[
\boxed{{}^{a}\mathbf v=\mathbf R_{ab}\,{}^{b}\mathbf v}.
\]

The subscript order can be read as:

\begin{quote}
\(\mathbf{R}_{ab}\) maps coordinates \textbf{from frame \(\{b\}\) to
frame \(\{a\}\)}.
\end{quote}

The \hyperref[sec-linear-algebra-foundations]{linear algebra foundations
chapter} develops orthogonal matrices, determinants, and inverses in
general. Applying those results to the orthonormal, right-handed columns
of \(\mathbf R_{ab}\) gives:

\[
\boxed{
\mathbf{R}_{ab}^\mathsf{T}\mathbf{R}_{ab}=\mathbf{I}_3,
\qquad
\det(\mathbf{R}_{ab})=1,
\qquad
\mathbf{R}_{ab}^{-1}=\mathbf{R}_{ab}^\mathsf{T}=\mathbf{R}_{ba}
}.
\]

The following derivations explain each property.

\subsubsection{Orthonormal columns}\label{orthonormal-columns}

Let \(\mathbf r_1,\mathbf r_2,\mathbf r_3\in\mathbb R^3\) denote the
columns of \(\mathbf R_{ab}\):

\[
\mathbf R_{ab}
=
\begin{bmatrix}
\mathbf r_1 & \mathbf r_2 & \mathbf r_3
\end{bmatrix},
\]

where \(\mathbf r_1={}^{a}\mathbf e_{x,b}\),
\(\mathbf r_2={}^{a}\mathbf e_{y,b}\), and
\(\mathbf r_3={}^{a}\mathbf e_{z,b}\). Multiplying the transpose by the
original matrix forms every pairwise dot product between these columns:

\[
\mathbf R_{ab}^{\mathsf T}\mathbf R_{ab}
=
\begin{bmatrix}
\mathbf r_1^{\mathsf T}\mathbf r_1 & \mathbf r_1^{\mathsf T}\mathbf r_2 & \mathbf r_1^{\mathsf T}\mathbf r_3\\
\mathbf r_2^{\mathsf T}\mathbf r_1 & \mathbf r_2^{\mathsf T}\mathbf r_2 & \mathbf r_2^{\mathsf T}\mathbf r_3\\
\mathbf r_3^{\mathsf T}\mathbf r_1 & \mathbf r_3^{\mathsf T}\mathbf r_2 & \mathbf r_3^{\mathsf T}\mathbf r_3
\end{bmatrix}.
\]

Because the frame axes are orthonormal, every column has unit length,

\[
\mathbf r_i^{\mathsf T}\mathbf r_i=1,
\]

and distinct columns are perpendicular,

\[
\mathbf r_i^{\mathsf T}\mathbf r_j=0
\qquad\text{for }i\neq j,
\]

where \(i,j\in\{1,2,3\}\) are column indices. Therefore,

\[
\boxed{
\mathbf R_{ab}^{\mathsf T}\mathbf R_{ab}
=
\begin{bmatrix}
1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1
\end{bmatrix}
=
\mathbf I_3
}.
\]

This identity means that \(\mathbf R_{ab}\) preserves dot products and
therefore preserves vector lengths and angles.

\subsubsection{Inverse and reverse coordinate
mapping}\label{inverse-and-reverse-coordinate-mapping}

The inverse \(\mathbf R_{ab}^{-1}\in\mathbb R^{3\times3}\) is defined by

\[
\mathbf R_{ab}^{-1}\mathbf R_{ab}=\mathbf I_3.
\]

Because \(\mathbf R_{ab}^{\mathsf T}\mathbf R_{ab}=\mathbf I_3\), the
transpose satisfies the defining property of the inverse. Hence,

\[
\boxed{\mathbf R_{ab}^{-1}=\mathbf R_{ab}^{\mathsf T}}.
\]

The coordinate transformation from frame \(\{b\}\) to frame \(\{a\}\) is

\[
{}^{a}\mathbf v=\mathbf R_{ab}\,{}^{b}\mathbf v.
\]

Multiplying by the inverse gives

\[
{}^{b}\mathbf v=\mathbf R_{ab}^{-1}\,{}^{a}\mathbf v.
\]

Define \(\mathbf R_{ba}\in\mathbb R^{3\times3}\) as the matrix that maps
coordinates in the reverse direction, from frame \(\{a\}\) to frame
\(\{b\}\). By definition,

\[
{}^{b}\mathbf v=\mathbf R_{ba}\,{}^{a}\mathbf v.
\]

The two reverse mappings must be the same, so

\[
\boxed{
\mathbf R_{ba}
=
\mathbf R_{ab}^{-1}
=
\mathbf R_{ab}^{\mathsf T}
}.
\]

\subsubsection{Determinant and
orientation}\label{determinant-and-orientation}

The notation \(\det(\mathbf R)\) denotes the \textbf{determinant} of a
square matrix \(\mathbf R\). Geometrically, \(|\det(\mathbf R)|\) is the
volume-scaling factor of the corresponding linear transformation. For an
orthogonal matrix \(\mathbf R\), taking determinants of
\(\mathbf R^{\mathsf T}\mathbf R=\mathbf I_3\) gives

\[
\det(\mathbf R^{\mathsf T}\mathbf R)
=
\det(\mathbf I_3),
\]

and therefore by
\(\det(\mathbf R^{\mathsf T}\mathbf R) = \det(\mathbf R^{\mathsf T})\det(\mathbf R)\)
and \(\det(R^{\mathsf T})=\det(\mathbf R)\) gives

\[
\det(\mathbf R)^2=1.
\]

Thus, an orthogonal matrix can have determinant \(+1\) or \(-1\). Here,
\textbf{orientation} means the handedness of the ordered coordinate
axes. A right-handed frame satisfies

\[
\mathbf e_x\times\mathbf e_y=\mathbf e_z.
\]

The determinant sign has the following interpretation:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(\det(\mathbf R)=+1\) preserves handedness, so a right-handed ordered
  basis remains right-handed.
\item
  \(\det(\mathbf R)=-1\) reverses handedness, so the transformation
  contains a reflection and produces a left-handed ordered basis from a
  right-handed one.
\end{itemize}

For example, consider

\[
\mathbf M
=
\begin{bmatrix}
-1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1
\end{bmatrix}.
\]

The matrix \(\mathbf M\in\mathbb R^{3\times3}\) reflects the
\(x\)-coordinate. Its columns remain orthonormal, so
\(\mathbf M^{\mathsf T}\mathbf M=\mathbf I_3\), but
\(\det(\mathbf M)=-1\). It reverses handedness and is therefore a
reflection rather than a rigid-body rotation.

Because frames \(\{a\}\) and \(\{b\}\) are both right-handed, their
rotation matrix must satisfy

\[
\boxed{
\mathbf R_{ab}^{\mathsf T}\mathbf R_{ab}=\mathbf I_3
\quad\text{and}\quad
\det(\mathbf R_{ab})=1
}.
\]

The determinant sign does not indicate whether a rotation is clockwise
or counter-clockwise. It indicates whether the transformation preserves
or reverses the handedness of space.

\subsection{Points require
translation}\label{points-require-translation}

Let \(O_a\) and \(O_b\) be the origins of frames \(\{a\}\) and
\(\{b\}\). Define \({}^{a}\mathbf p_b\in\mathbb R^3\) as the position of
\(O_b\) relative to \(O_a\), expressed in frame \(\{a\}\) and measured
in metres.

For a physical point \(P\), let \({}^{b}\mathbf p_P\in\mathbb R^3\) be
the position of \(P\) relative to \(O_b\), expressed in frame \(\{b\}\),
and let \({}^{a}\mathbf p_P\in\mathbb R^3\) be the position of \(P\)
relative to \(O_a\), expressed in frame \(\{a\}\). Both position columns
are measured in metres. Their relationship is

\[
{}^{a}\mathbf{p}_P
=
\mathbf{R}_{ab}\,{}^{b}\mathbf{p}_P
+{}^{a}\mathbf{p}_b.
\]

The rotation \(\mathbf R_{ab}\,{}^{b}\mathbf p_P\) changes the basis
used to express the displacement from \(O_b\) to \(P\). The translation
\({}^{a}\mathbf p_b\) then accounts for the displacement from \(O_a\) to
\(O_b\). A homogeneous transformation combines these two operations into
one matrix equation.

\section{Worked frame-change example}\label{worked-frame-change-example}

Consider two planar frames \(\{a\}\) and \(\{b\}\) whose axes differ by
an angle \(\theta\in\mathbb R\), measured in radians. A positive
\(\theta\) means that frame \(\{b\}\) is rotated counter-clockwise
relative to frame \(\{a\}\). Define
\(\mathbf R_{ab}(\theta)\in\mathbb R^{2\times2}\) as the rotation matrix
that maps two-dimensional coordinate columns from frame \(\{b\}\) to
frame \(\{a\}\):

\[
{}^{a}\mathbf v
=
\mathbf R_{ab}(\theta)\,{}^{b}\mathbf v,
\]

where \(\mathbf v\) is a geometric free vector and
\({}^{a}\mathbf v,{}^{b}\mathbf v\in\mathbb R^2\) are its coordinate
columns in frames \(\{a\}\) and \(\{b\}\).

Let \(\mathbf e_{x,a}\) and \(\mathbf e_{y,a}\) be the geometric unit
axes of frame \(\{a\}\), and let \(\mathbf e_{x,b}\) and
\(\mathbf e_{y,b}\) be the geometric unit axes of frame \(\{b\}\). By
definition, the columns of \(\mathbf R_{ab}\) are the axes of frame
\(\{b\}\) expressed in frame \(\{a\}\):

\[
\mathbf R_{ab}
=
\begin{bmatrix}
{}^{a}\mathbf e_{x,b}&{}^{a}\mathbf e_{y,b}
\end{bmatrix}.
\]

The \(x_b\)-axis makes angle \(\theta\) with the \(x_a\)-axis. Its
projection onto \(\mathbf e_{x,a}\) is \(\cos\theta\), and its
projection onto \(\mathbf e_{y,a}\) is \(\sin\theta\). Therefore,

\[
{}^{a}\mathbf e_{x,b}
=
\begin{bmatrix}
\cos\theta\\
\sin\theta
\end{bmatrix}.
\]

Because the planar frames are right-handed and orthonormal, the
\(y_b\)-axis is obtained by rotating the \(x_b\)-axis counter-clockwise
by \(\pi/2\). Its angle measured from the \(x_a\)-axis is therefore
\(\theta+\pi/2\), so

\[
{}^{a}\mathbf e_{y,b}
=
\begin{bmatrix}
\cos(\theta+\pi/2)\\
\sin(\theta+\pi/2)
\end{bmatrix}.
\]

Using

\[
\cos(\theta+\pi/2)=-\sin\theta,
\qquad
\sin(\theta+\pi/2)=\cos\theta,
\]

gives

\[
{}^{a}\mathbf e_{y,b}
=
\begin{bmatrix}
-\sin\theta\\
\cos\theta
\end{bmatrix}.
\]

Placing these two coordinate columns side by side produces

\[
\boxed{
\mathbf R_{ab}(\theta)
=
\begin{bmatrix}
\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\
\sin\theta&\cos\theta
\end{bmatrix}
}.
\]

The column construction also explains why this matrix maps coordinates
from frame \(\{b\}\) to frame \(\{a\}\). If

\[
{}^{b}\mathbf v
=
\begin{bmatrix}
v_x^{(b)}\\
v_y^{(b)}
\end{bmatrix},
\]

where \(v_x^{(b)},v_y^{(b)}\in\mathbb R\) are the components of
\(\mathbf v\) along the \(x_b\)- and \(y_b\)-axes, then

\[
\begin{aligned}
{}^{a}\mathbf v
&=v_x^{(b)}\,{}^{a}\mathbf e_{x,b}
+v_y^{(b)}\,{}^{a}\mathbf e_{y,b}\\
&=
\begin{bmatrix}
{}^{a}\mathbf e_{x,b}&{}^{a}\mathbf e_{y,b}
\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}
v_x^{(b)}\\
v_y^{(b)}
\end{bmatrix}\\
&=\mathbf R_{ab}(\theta)\,{}^{b}\mathbf v.
\end{aligned}
\]

For the worked example, \(\theta=\pi/2\,\mathrm{rad}\). Substitution
gives

\[
\mathbf R_{ab}(\pi/2)
=
\begin{bmatrix}
0&-1\\
1&0
\end{bmatrix}.
\]

The first column \([0\;1]^{\mathsf T}\) says that the positive
\(x_b\)-axis points along the positive \(y_a\)-axis. The second column
\([-1\;0]^{\mathsf T}\) says that the positive \(y_b\)-axis points along
the negative \(x_a\)-axis.

Let \({}^{b}\mathbf v\in\mathbb R^2\) be the coordinate column of a free
vector \(\mathbf v\), expressed in frame \(\{b\}\) and measured in
metres:

\[
{}^{b}\mathbf{v}
=
\begin{bmatrix}
2\\
1
\end{bmatrix}
\mathrm{m}.
\]

The coordinate column of the same geometric vector in frame \(\{a\}\) is
\({}^{a}\mathbf v\in\mathbb R^2\):

\[
{}^{a}\mathbf{v}
=
\mathbf{R}_{ab}(\theta)\,{}^{b}\mathbf{v}
=
\begin{bmatrix}
0 & -1\\
1 & 0
\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}
2\\
1
\end{bmatrix}
=
\begin{bmatrix}
-1\\
2
\end{bmatrix}
\mathrm{m}.
\]

The coordinates changed, but the physical length did not:

\[
\left\|{}^{b}\mathbf{v}\right\|_2
=
\sqrt{2^2+1^2}
=
\sqrt{5}
=
\sqrt{(-1)^2+2^2}
=
\left\|{}^{a}\mathbf{v}\right\|_2.
\]

\subsection{Interactive frame-rotation
graph}\label{interactive-frame-rotation-graph}

Fix the frame-\(\{b\}\) coordinate column

\[
{}^{b}\mathbf v
=
\begin{bmatrix}
2\\
1
\end{bmatrix}.
\]

For each angle \(\theta\in[-\pi,\pi]\), the corresponding
frame-\(\{a\}\) coordinate column is

\[
{}^{a}\mathbf v(\theta)
=
\mathbf R_{ab}(\theta)\,{}^{b}\mathbf v
=
\begin{bmatrix}
2\cos\theta-\sin\theta\\
2\sin\theta+\cos\theta
\end{bmatrix}.
\]

In the HTML book, move the angle slider to rotate frame \(\{b\}\)
relative to frame \(\{a\}\). The graph keeps
\({}^{b}\mathbf v=[2\;1]^{\mathsf T}\) fixed, so the geometric vector
rotates with frame \(\{b\}\) and its coordinates
\({}^{a}\mathbf v(\theta)\) update continuously.

The PDF shows the default slider state \(\theta=\pi/2\,\mathrm{rad}\).
Use the HTML book to vary \(\theta\) interactively.

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\includegraphics[width=0.85\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{index_files/mediabag/figures/planar_frame_rotation_static.pdf}

}

\caption{\label{fig-planar-frame-rotation-static}Static PDF view of the
frame rotation at \(\theta=\pi/2\) with
\({}^{b}\mathbf v=[2\;1]^{\mathsf T}\) and
\({}^{a}\mathbf v=[-1\;2]^{\mathsf T}\).}

\end{figure}%

\subsubsection{Equivalent NumPy
calculation}\label{equivalent-numpy-calculation}

The following NumPy snippet is the direct software translation of
\({}^{a}\mathbf v=\mathbf R_{ab}(\theta){}^{b}\mathbf v\). The scalar
\texttt{theta} stores \(\theta\) in radians; \texttt{R\_ab} is a NumPy
array with shape \texttt{(2,\ 2)} representing
\(\mathbf R_{ab}(\theta)\in\mathbb R^{2\times2}\); \texttt{v\_b} is a
NumPy array with shape \texttt{(2,)} representing the fixed coordinate
column \({}^{b}\mathbf v=[2\;1]^{\mathsf T}\); and \texttt{v\_a} is a
NumPy array with shape \texttt{(2,)} representing
\({}^{a}\mathbf v\in\mathbb R^2\). The operator \texttt{@} performs
matrix-vector multiplication.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ numpy }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ np}

\NormalTok{theta }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.pi }\OperatorTok{/} \DecValTok{2}  \CommentTok{\# radians; replace this value with the selected angle}

\NormalTok{R\_ab }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.array([}
\NormalTok{    [np.cos(theta), }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{np.sin(theta)],}
\NormalTok{    [np.sin(theta),  np.cos(theta)],}
\NormalTok{])}

\NormalTok{v\_b }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.array([}\FloatTok{2.0}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.0}\NormalTok{])}
\NormalTok{v\_a }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ R\_ab }\OperatorTok{@}\NormalTok{ v\_b}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

For \(\theta=\pi/2\,\mathrm{rad}\), the calculation gives \texttt{v\_a}
approximately equal to \texttt{{[}-1.0,\ 2.0{]}}. Changing
\texttt{theta} recomputes the same values displayed by the interactive
graph while \texttt{v\_b} remains fixed.

\section{Differential-drive
interpretation}\label{differential-drive-interpretation}

For a planar mobile robot, let frame \(\{w\}\) be fixed to the world and
frame \(\{b\}\) be attached to the robot. Let \(\theta\in\mathbb R\) be
the robot's heading angle, measured counter-clockwise in radians from
the positive \(x_w\)-axis to the positive \(x_b\)-axis. Let
\(v\in\mathbb R\) be the signed forward speed, measured in
\(\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\); positive \(v\) means motion along the positive
\(x_b\)-axis.

The body-frame velocity \({}^{b}\mathbf v\in\mathbb R^2\) is

\[
{}^{b}\mathbf{v}
=
\begin{bmatrix}
v\\
0
\end{bmatrix}
\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}.
\]

Define \(\mathbf R_{wb}(\theta)\in\mathbb R^{2\times2}\) as the rotation
matrix that maps planar coordinates from the robot frame \(\{b\}\) to
the world frame \(\{w\}\). The world-frame velocity
\({}^{w}\mathbf v\in\mathbb R^2\) is therefore

\[
{}^{w}\mathbf{v}
=
\mathbf{R}_{wb}(\theta)\,{}^{b}\mathbf{v}
=
\begin{bmatrix}
\cos\theta & -\sin\theta\\
\sin\theta & \cos\theta
\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}
v\\
0
\end{bmatrix}
=
\begin{bmatrix}
v\cos\theta\\
v\sin\theta
\end{bmatrix}
\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}.
\]

This equation is the coordinate-frame foundation of the
differential-drive kinematic model. It shows why a controller must
distinguish a forward command in the robot frame from motion observed in
the world frame.

\section{Failure modes and engineering
checks}\label{failure-modes-and-engineering-checks}

\subsection{Missing frame labels}\label{missing-frame-labels}

Two identical columns can describe different physical directions when
they are expressed in different frames. Interfaces should name the frame
explicitly.

\subsection{Reversed transformation}\label{reversed-transformation}

Using \(\mathbf{R}_{ba}\) where \(\mathbf{R}_{ab}\) is required rotates
coordinates in the opposite direction. Check the subscript rule before
multiplying.

\subsection{Mixed units}\label{mixed-units}

Degrees passed to a function expecting radians produce valid-looking but
wrong numbers. Convert at the system boundary and use radians
internally.

\subsection{Point-vector confusion}\label{point-vector-confusion}

A point changes under translation; a free vector does not. Applying a
translation to velocity or direction data is a modelling error.

\subsection{Invalid numerical
rotation}\label{invalid-numerical-rotation}

Let \(\mathbf R\in\mathbb R^{n\times n}\) be a numerically stored matrix
intended to represent a rotation, and let
\(\mathbf I_n\in\mathbb R^{n\times n}\) be the identity matrix.
Rounding, integration drift, or corrupted data can make \(\mathbf R\)
non-orthonormal.

For a matrix \(\mathbf M\in\mathbb R^{n\times n}\) with entries
\(m_{ij}\), define the Frobenius norm by

\[
\lVert\mathbf M\rVert_F
\mathrel{:=}
\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}m_{ij}^2}.
\]

Let \(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{orth}}>0\) and \(\varepsilon_{\det}>0\) be
declared numerical tolerances. A robust interface checks

\[
\left\lVert\mathbf{R}^\mathsf{T}\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{I}_n\right\rVert_F
\leq
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{orth}}
\]

and

\[
\left|\det(\mathbf R)-1\right|
\leq
\varepsilon_{\det}.
\]

The first condition tests approximate orthonormality; the second tests
approximate preservation of right-handed orientation.

\section*{Quick test B: coordinate frames and
rotations}\label{quick-test-b-coordinate-frames-and-rotations}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quick test B: coordinate frames and
rotations}

\markright{Quick test B: coordinate frames and rotations}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Recall:} What do the columns of \(\mathbf{R}_{ab}\) represent?
\item
  \textbf{Explanation:} Explain in words why
  \(\mathbf{R}_{ab}^{-1}=\mathbf{R}_{ba}\).
\item
  \textbf{Derivation:} Starting with
  \({}^{a}\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{R}_{ab}{}^{b}\mathbf{v}\), solve for
  \({}^{b}\mathbf{v}\).
\item
  \textbf{Application:} A robot has heading \(\theta=\pi/2\) and forward
  speed \(v=0.5\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\). Find its world-frame velocity.
\item
  \textbf{Limitations:} Give two ways a numerically stored matrix
  intended to be a rotation matrix can become invalid.
\item
  \textbf{Derivation:} Let \(\{a\}\) and \(\{b\}\) be right-handed
  orthonormal planar frames. Frame \(\{b\}\) is rotated
  counter-clockwise by \(\theta\,\mathrm{rad}\) relative to frame
  \(\{a\}\), and \(\mathbf R_{ab}(\theta)\in\mathbb R^{2\times2}\) maps
  coordinate columns from frame \(\{b\}\) to frame \(\{a\}\). First
  express the \(x_b\)-axis and \(y_b\)-axis as coordinate columns
  \({}^{a}\mathbf e_{x,b},{}^{a}\mathbf e_{y,b}\in\mathbb R^2\); then
  place those columns side by side to derive \(\mathbf R_{ab}(\theta)\).
\end{enumerate}

\subsection*{Answers and hints}\label{answers-and-hints-5}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Answers and hints}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  The axes of frame \(\{b\}\) expressed in coordinates of frame
  \(\{a\}\).
\item
  Reversing the frame change undoes the original change; for an
  orthonormal rotation matrix, the inverse is its transpose.
\item
  Premultiply by \(\mathbf{R}_{ab}^{-1}=\mathbf{R}_{ba}\):
  \({}^{b}\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{R}_{ba}{}^{a}\mathbf{v}\).
\item
  \([0\;\;0.5]^\mathsf{T}\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\), up to floating-point
  rounding.
\item
  Examples include accumulated integration error, rounded entries,
  malformed input, unit mistakes in angle construction, and data
  corruption.
\item
  Since the \(x_b\)-axis makes angle \(\theta\) with the \(x_a\)-axis,
  its frame-\(\{a\}\) coordinate column is
  \({}^{a}\mathbf e_{x,b}=[\cos\theta\;\sin\theta]^{\mathsf T}\).
  Right-handed orthonormality places the \(y_b\)-axis at angle
  \(\theta+\pi/2\), so
  \({}^{a}\mathbf e_{y,b}=[\cos(\theta+\pi/2)\;\sin(\theta+\pi/2)]^{\mathsf T}=[-\sin\theta\;\cos\theta]^{\mathsf T}\).
  Because these two coordinate columns are the columns of
  \(\mathbf R_{ab}(\theta)\),

  \[
  \mathbf R_{ab}(\theta)
  =
  \begin{bmatrix}
  \cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\
  \sin\theta&\cos\theta
  \end{bmatrix}.
  \]
\end{enumerate}
