\chapter{Differential-Drive Kinematics and Numerical Integration:
Implementation}\label{sec-kinematics-integration-implementation}

\section*{Purpose and scope}\label{purpose-and-scope}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Purpose and scope}

\markright{Purpose and scope}

This companion translates the differential-drive mathematics derived in
\hyperref[sec-differential-drive-model]{Differential-Drive Kinematics
and Numerical Integration: Model and Derivation} into reusable software
contracts, invariants, validation rules, and tests. It records how those
equations map into dependable software without repeating the
derivations.

\section{Wheel-to-body velocity
mapping}\label{wheel-to-body-velocity-mapping}

Let \(r>0\) be the common effective wheel radius in metres and let
\(L>0\) be the track width in metres. Let \(\dot\phi_L\) and
\(\dot\phi_R\) be the signed left and right wheel angular speeds in
\(\mathrm{rad\,s^{-1}}\), where positive angular speed rolls the
corresponding wheel forward.

The software implements

\[
v
=
\frac{r}{2}
\left(\dot\phi_R+\dot\phi_L\right),
\qquad
\omega
=
\frac{r}{L}
\left(\dot\phi_R-\dot\phi_L\right),
\]

where \(v\) is signed body-forward speed in \(\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\) and
\(\omega\) is signed counter-clockwise yaw rate in
\(\mathrm{rad\,s^{-1}}\).

\subsection{Software contract}\label{software-contract}

The \texttt{wheel\_to\_body} operation accepts four finite scalar
inputs: left wheel angular speed, right wheel angular speed, wheel
radius, and track width. The wheel radius and track width must be
strictly positive. Invalid inputs raise \texttt{ValueError}; the
operation does not clamp values or substitute default geometry.

The operation returns an immutable \texttt{BodyVelocity} value
containing \texttt{forward\_speed\_m\_s} and \texttt{yaw\_rate\_rad\_s}.
It is deterministic, does not mutate its inputs, and does not depend on
ROS state, timestamps, configuration files, or hidden global state.

\subsection{Simplified reference
snippet}\label{simplified-reference-snippet}

The following equation-focused snippet shows how the mathematical
factors map to Python expressions. It assumes that all inputs are finite
and that \texttt{wheel\_radius\_m} and \texttt{track\_width\_m} are
strictly positive; the production operation enforces those preconditions
before performing the calculation.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\AttributeTok{@dataclass}\NormalTok{(frozen}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{, slots}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ BodyVelocity:}
    \CommentTok{"""Reduced planar body velocity in SI units."""}

\NormalTok{    forward\_speed\_m\_s: }\BuiltInTok{float}
\NormalTok{    yaw\_rate\_rad\_s: }\BuiltInTok{float}


\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ wheel\_to\_body(}
    \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    left\_angular\_speed\_rad\_s: }\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    right\_angular\_speed\_rad\_s: }\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    wheel\_radius\_m: }\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    track\_width\_m: }\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ BodyVelocity:}
    \CommentTok{"""Apply the wheel{-}to{-}body equations to validated inputs."""}
\NormalTok{    forward\_speed\_m\_s }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (}
\NormalTok{        wheel\_radius\_m }\OperatorTok{/} \DecValTok{2}
        \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ (right\_angular\_speed\_rad\_s }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ left\_angular\_speed\_rad\_s)}
\NormalTok{    )}
\NormalTok{    yaw\_rate\_rad\_s }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (}
\NormalTok{        wheel\_radius\_m }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ track\_width\_m}
        \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ (right\_angular\_speed\_rad\_s }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ left\_angular\_speed\_rad\_s)}
\NormalTok{    )}

    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ BodyVelocity(}
\NormalTok{        forward\_speed\_m\_s}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{forward\_speed\_m\_s,}
\NormalTok{        yaw\_rate\_rad\_s}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{yaw\_rate\_rad\_s,}
\NormalTok{    )}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{Invariants}\label{invariants}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Equal wheel speeds produce zero yaw rate.
\item
  Equal and opposite wheel speeds produce zero forward speed.
\item
  Swapping the left and right wheel speeds preserves forward speed and
  reverses yaw rate.
\item
  Two zero wheel speeds produce zero forward speed and zero yaw rate.
\item
  Positive right-wheel speed relative to left-wheel speed produces
  positive counter-clockwise yaw.
\end{itemize}

\section{Body-to-wheel velocity
mapping}\label{body-to-wheel-velocity-mapping}

The \texttt{body\_to\_wheel} operation implements the inverse of the
wheel-to-body mapping. Its inputs are signed body-forward speed \(v\) in
\(\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\), signed counter-clockwise yaw rate \(\omega\) in
\(\mathrm{rad\,s^{-1}}\), wheel radius \(r>0\) in metres, and track
width \(L>0\) in metres.

The required wheel angular speeds are

\[
\dot\phi_L
=
\frac{v}{r}
-
\frac{L\omega}{2r},
\qquad
\dot\phi_R
=
\frac{v}{r}
+
\frac{L\omega}{2r},
\]

where \(\dot\phi_L\) and \(\dot\phi_R\) are the signed left and right
wheel angular speeds in \(\mathrm{rad\,s^{-1}}\).

\subsection{Software contract}\label{software-contract-1}

The operation accepts four finite scalar inputs. The wheel radius and
track width must be strictly positive. Invalid inputs raise
\texttt{ValueError}; the operation does not clamp values or substitute
geometry.

The result is an immutable \texttt{WheelAngularSpeeds} value containing
\texttt{left\_angular\_speed\_rad\_s} and
\texttt{right\_angular\_speed\_rad\_s}.

\subsection{Implementation mapping}\label{implementation-mapping}

The calculation separates forward and turning contributions:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\AttributeTok{@dataclass}\NormalTok{(frozen}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{, slots}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ WheelAngularSpeeds:}
    \CommentTok{"""Signed left and right wheel angular speeds in radians per second."""}

\NormalTok{    left\_angular\_speed\_rad\_s: }\BuiltInTok{float}
\NormalTok{    right\_angular\_speed\_rad\_s: }\BuiltInTok{float}


\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ body\_to\_wheel(}
    \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    forward\_speed\_m\_s: }\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    yaw\_rate\_rad\_s: }\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    wheel\_radius\_m: }\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    track\_width\_m: }\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ WheelAngularSpeeds:}
    \CommentTok{"""Apply the body{-}to{-}wheel equations to validated inputs."""}
\NormalTok{    forward\_component\_rad\_s }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ forward\_speed\_m\_s }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ wheel\_radius\_m}
\NormalTok{    turning\_component\_rad\_s }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (}
\NormalTok{        track\_width\_m }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ yaw\_rate\_rad\_s }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ wheel\_radius\_m)}
\NormalTok{    )}
\NormalTok{    left\_angular\_speed\_rad\_s }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (}
\NormalTok{        forward\_component\_rad\_s }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ turning\_component\_rad\_s}
\NormalTok{    )}
\NormalTok{    right\_angular\_speed\_rad\_s }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (}
\NormalTok{        forward\_component\_rad\_s }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ turning\_component\_rad\_s}
\NormalTok{    )}

    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ WheelAngularSpeeds(}
\NormalTok{        left\_angular\_speed\_rad\_s}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{left\_angular\_speed\_rad\_s,}
\NormalTok{        right\_angular\_speed\_rad\_s}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{right\_angular\_speed\_rad\_s,}
\NormalTok{    )}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The forward component contributes equally to both wheels. The turning
component is subtracted from the left wheel and added to the right
wheel. Therefore, a positive yaw rate makes the right wheel turn faster
than the left wheel.

\subsection{Invariants and required
tests}\label{invariants-and-required-tests}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Zero yaw rate produces equal wheel speeds of \(v/r\).
\item
  Zero forward speed produces equal-and-opposite wheel speeds.
\item
  Reversing the yaw-rate sign swaps the left and right wheel speeds.
\item
  Applying \texttt{body\_to\_wheel} and then \texttt{wheel\_to\_body}
  recovers the original \(v\) and \(\omega\) within the declared
  floating-point tolerance.
\item
  Non-finite inputs and non-positive geometry raise \texttt{ValueError}.
\end{itemize}

\section{Exact constant-input pose
integration}\label{exact-constant-input-pose-integration}

Let the initial planar pose be \((x_k,y_k,\theta_k)\), where \(x_k\) and
\(y_k\) are the world-frame coordinates of the axle midpoint in metres
and \(\theta_k\) is the unwrapped counter-clockwise heading in radians.

Assume that forward speed \(v\) in \(\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\) and yaw rate
\(\omega\) in \(\mathrm{rad\,s^{-1}}\) remain constant over a time step
\(\Delta t>0\) in seconds. Define the heading change

\[
\Delta\theta
=
\omega\Delta t.
\]

The exact update is

\[
\begin{aligned}
x_{k+1}
&=
x_k+
v\Delta t\,
\operatorname{sinc}\left(\frac{\Delta\theta}{2}\right)
\cos\left(\theta_k+\frac{\Delta\theta}{2}\right),\\
y_{k+1}
&=
y_k+
v\Delta t\,
\operatorname{sinc}\left(\frac{\Delta\theta}{2}\right)
\sin\left(\theta_k+\frac{\Delta\theta}{2}\right),\\
\theta_{k+1}
&=
\theta_k+\Delta\theta,
\end{aligned}
\]

where the unnormalised sinc function is

\[
\operatorname{sinc}(z)
=
\begin{cases}
\dfrac{\sin z}{z}, & z\neq0,\\[4pt]
1, & z=0.
\end{cases}
\]

The update is exact for the ideal continuous kinematic model only when
\(v\) and \(\omega\) remain constant during the time step. It does not
account for wheel slip, changing commands, actuator dynamics, or model
error.

\subsection{Software contract}\label{software-contract-2}

The \texttt{integrate\_exact} operation accepts an immutable
\texttt{PlanarPose}, finite forward speed, finite yaw rate, and a finite
positive time step. A non-finite input or non-positive time step raises
\texttt{ValueError}.

The operation returns a new \texttt{PlanarPose}. It does not mutate the
initial pose or wrap the resulting heading into a fixed angular
interval.

\subsection{Numerically stable implementation
mapping}\label{numerically-stable-implementation-mapping}

The implementation avoids evaluating \(v/\omega\), which would be
undefined when \(\omega=0\). Instead, it uses the sinc form:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\AttributeTok{@dataclass}\NormalTok{(frozen}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{, slots}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ PlanarPose:}
    \CommentTok{"""Pose of the robot\textquotesingle{}s axle midpoint in the world frame.}
\CommentTok{    }
\CommentTok{    Attributes:}
\CommentTok{        position\_x\_m: World{-}frame x{-}coordinate in metres.}
\CommentTok{        position\_y\_m: World{-}frame y{-}coordinate in metres.}
\CommentTok{        heading\_rad: Counter{-}clockwise heading from the world{-}frame}
\CommentTok{            positive x{-}axis in radians.}
\CommentTok{    """}

\NormalTok{    position\_x\_m: }\BuiltInTok{float}
\NormalTok{    position\_y\_m: }\BuiltInTok{float}
\NormalTok{    heading\_rad: }\BuiltInTok{float}
    

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ \_sinc(value: }\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{:}
    \CommentTok{"""Return the unnormalised sinc function, including its value at zero."""}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ value }\OperatorTok{==} \FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{:}
        \ControlFlowTok{return} \FloatTok{1.0}

    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ sin(value) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ value}


\NormalTok{heading\_change\_rad }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ yaw\_rate\_rad\_s }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ time\_step\_s}
\NormalTok{half\_heading\_change\_rad }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ heading\_change\_rad }\OperatorTok{/} \DecValTok{2}
\NormalTok{midpoint\_heading\_rad }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ pose.heading\_rad }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ half\_heading\_change\_rad}

\NormalTok{signed\_displacement\_m }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (}
\NormalTok{    forward\_speed\_m\_s}
    \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ time\_step\_s}
    \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ \_sinc(half\_heading\_change\_rad)}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{next\_pose }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ PlanarPose(}
\NormalTok{    position\_x\_m}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}
\NormalTok{        pose.position\_x\_m}
        \OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ signed\_displacement\_m }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ cos(midpoint\_heading\_rad)}
\NormalTok{    ),}
\NormalTok{    position\_y\_m}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}
\NormalTok{        pose.position\_y\_m}
        \OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ signed\_displacement\_m }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ sin(midpoint\_heading\_rad)}
\NormalTok{    ),}
\NormalTok{    heading\_rad}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{pose.heading\_rad }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ heading\_change\_rad,}
\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

When \(\omega=0\), we have \(\Delta\theta=0\) and
\(\operatorname{sinc}(0)=1\). The same implementation therefore reduces
smoothly to straight motion without a separate division by zero.

\subsection{Invariants and required
tests}\label{invariants-and-required-tests-1}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Zero yaw rate produces the exact straight-motion update.
\item
  Zero forward speed changes only the heading.
\item
  Constant nonzero forward speed and yaw rate produce constant-curvature
  motion.
\item
  The input pose remains unchanged.
\item
  The output heading remains unwrapped.
\item
  Non-finite inputs raise \texttt{ValueError}.
\item
  A zero or negative time step raises \texttt{ValueError}.
\end{itemize}

\section{Forward Euler pose
integration}\label{forward-euler-pose-integration}

Forward Euler approximates motion over a time step \(\Delta t>0\) by
evaluating the pose derivative at the beginning of the step. For initial
pose \((x_k,y_k,\theta_k)\), constant forward speed \(v\), and constant
yaw rate \(\omega\), the update is

\[
\begin{aligned}
x_{k+1}^{\mathrm E}
&=
x_k+\Delta t\,v\cos\theta_k,\\
y_{k+1}^{\mathrm E}
&=
y_k+\Delta t\,v\sin\theta_k,\\
\theta_{k+1}^{\mathrm E}
&=
\theta_k+\Delta t\,\omega.
\end{aligned}
\]

The superscript \(\mathrm E\) identifies a Forward Euler approximation.
Position is calculated entirely from the beginning-of-step heading
\(\theta_k\); the changing heading within the step does not affect that
step's position increment.

\subsection{Software contract}\label{software-contract-3}

The \texttt{integrate\_forward\_euler} operation accepts the same inputs
and validation rules as \texttt{integrate\_exact}: an immutable
\texttt{PlanarPose}, finite forward speed, finite yaw rate, and a finite
positive time step.

The operation returns a new \texttt{PlanarPose}, does not mutate the
input pose, and leaves the heading unwrapped. Invalid inputs raise
\texttt{ValueError}.

\subsection{Implementation mapping}\label{implementation-mapping-1}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{next\_pose }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ PlanarPose(}
\NormalTok{    position\_x\_m}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}
\NormalTok{        pose.position\_x\_m}
        \OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ time\_step\_s }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ forward\_speed\_m\_s }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ cos(pose.heading\_rad)}
\NormalTok{    ),}
\NormalTok{    position\_y\_m}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}
\NormalTok{        pose.position\_y\_m}
        \OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ time\_step\_s }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ forward\_speed\_m\_s }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ sin(pose.heading\_rad)}
\NormalTok{    ),}
\NormalTok{    heading\_rad}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}
\NormalTok{        pose.heading\_rad }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ time\_step\_s }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ yaw\_rate\_rad\_s}
\NormalTok{    ),}
\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{Exact and approximate
cases}\label{exact-and-approximate-cases}

When \(\omega=0\), the heading remains constant. The world-frame
velocity is therefore constant, so one Forward Euler step produces the
exact straight-motion position.

When \(v=0\), position remains unchanged and the heading update is exact
because \(\dot\theta=\omega\) is constant.

When both \(v\neq0\) and \(\omega\neq0\), the true path curves during
the time step. Forward Euler instead follows the tangent defined by
\(\theta_k\), so its one-step position is generally approximate.

For repeated integration over a fixed duration, Forward Euler has global
error \(O(\Delta t)\). In this regime, halving \(\Delta t\) should
approximately halve the accumulated error.

\subsection{Invariants and required
tests}\label{invariants-and-required-tests-2}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Straight constant-speed motion agrees with the exact update.
\item
  Pure rotation changes only the heading.
\item
  A turning step uses the beginning-of-step heading.
\item
  Smaller time steps reduce accumulated turning-position error.
\item
  Halving the time step approximately halves global error in the
  first-order regime.
\item
  Non-finite inputs and non-positive time steps raise
  \texttt{ValueError}.
\end{itemize}

\chapter{ROS 2 Package Development
Workflow}\label{sec-ros2-package-development-workflow}

\section*{Goal}\label{goal}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Goal}

\markright{Goal}

This workflow creates, builds, runs, and tests a small ROS 2 Python
package from scratch. It uses \texttt{my\_robot\_package} as an example
package name and \texttt{my\_node} as an example executable name;
replace them in a real project.

A \textbf{workspace} holds packages developed and built together. A
\textbf{ROS 2 package} combines source code with the metadata needed for
building, installing, and discovery. An \textbf{executable} is a program
installed by a package and started with \texttt{ros2\ run}.

The generated \texttt{my\_node} executable only prints a message. It
demonstrates the package workflow but does not yet communicate with
other ROS 2 nodes.

\section{Source the ROS 2 underlay}\label{source-the-ros-2-underlay}

For Zsh, run:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\BuiltInTok{source}\NormalTok{ /opt/ros/jazzy/setup.zsh}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

For Bash, run:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\BuiltInTok{source}\NormalTok{ /opt/ros/jazzy/setup.bash}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Use the script matching the current shell.

\textbf{What happens:} \texttt{/opt/ros/jazzy} is the ROS 2 Jazzy
\textbf{underlay}, meaning the existing installation on which the new
workspace depends. Its setup script adds ROS installation prefixes,
Python packages, and executables to the current shell's search
environment.

The command uses \texttt{source} because the environment of the current
shell must change. If the script ran in a separate \textbf{child
process}, meaning a process started by the shell, its environment
changes would disappear when that child exited.

Verify the environment:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\BuiltInTok{printf} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}\%s\textbackslash{}n\textquotesingle{}} \StringTok{"}\VariableTok{$ROS\_DISTRO}\StringTok{"}
\BuiltInTok{command} \AttributeTok{{-}v}\NormalTok{ ros2}
\BuiltInTok{command} \AttributeTok{{-}v}\NormalTok{ colcon}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The expected observations are \texttt{jazzy} and executable paths for
\texttt{ros2} and \texttt{colcon}.

\section{Create a workspace}\label{create-a-workspace}

For a new standalone workspace, run:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{mkdir} \AttributeTok{{-}p}\NormalTok{ \textasciitilde{}/ros\_ws/src}
\BuiltInTok{cd}\NormalTok{ \textasciitilde{}/ros\_ws}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The workspace develops into:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ros\_ws/}
\NormalTok{├── src/       \# Editable source}
\NormalTok{├── build/     \# Generated build workspace}
\NormalTok{├── install/   \# Generated runnable workspace image}
\NormalTok{└── log/       \# Generated build and test records}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Only \texttt{src/} exists initially. \texttt{colcon} creates the other
directories. Edit \texttt{src/}, not the generated files in
\texttt{build/}, \texttt{install/}, or \texttt{log/}.

\section{Create a Python package}\label{create-a-python-package}

From the workspace root, run:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\BuiltInTok{cd}\NormalTok{ src}
\ExtensionTok{ros2}\NormalTok{ pkg create }\DataTypeTok{\textbackslash{}}
  \AttributeTok{{-}{-}build{-}type}\NormalTok{ ament\_python }\DataTypeTok{\textbackslash{}}
  \AttributeTok{{-}{-}license}\NormalTok{ Apache{-}2.0 }\DataTypeTok{\textbackslash{}}
  \AttributeTok{{-}{-}node{-}name}\NormalTok{ my\_node }\DataTypeTok{\textbackslash{}}
\NormalTok{  my\_robot\_package}
\BuiltInTok{cd}\NormalTok{ ..}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Run this command only when the package does not already exist.

\textbf{What happens:} \texttt{ros2\ pkg\ create} generates
\texttt{src/my\_robot\_package}.'

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{ament\_python} selects Python packaging through
  \texttt{setuptools}.
\item
  \texttt{Apache-2.0} is recorded in \texttt{package.xml}, and a
  matching \texttt{LICENSE} file is created.
\item
  \texttt{-\/-node-name\ my\_node} creates a minimal Python executable
  and its \texttt{setup.py} entry point.
\end{itemize}

Omit \texttt{-\/-node-name} when creating a library-only package.

For comparison, create a C++ package with:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ExtensionTok{ros2}\NormalTok{ pkg create }\DataTypeTok{\textbackslash{}}
  \AttributeTok{{-}{-}build{-}type}\NormalTok{ ament\_cmake }\DataTypeTok{\textbackslash{}}
  \AttributeTok{{-}{-}license}\NormalTok{ Apache{-}2.0 }\DataTypeTok{\textbackslash{}}
\NormalTok{  robot\_controller}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The common build types are \texttt{ament\_python} for Python with
\texttt{setuptools} and \texttt{ament\_cmake} for C++ with CMake.

\section{Inspect the generated
package}\label{inspect-the-generated-package}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{src/my\_robot\_package/}
\NormalTok{├── LICENSE}
\NormalTok{├── package.xml}
\NormalTok{├── setup.py}
\NormalTok{├── setup.cfg}
\NormalTok{├── resource/}
\NormalTok{│   └── my\_robot\_package}
\NormalTok{├── my\_robot\_package/}
\NormalTok{│   ├── \_\_init\_\_.py}
\NormalTok{│   └── my\_node.py}
\NormalTok{└── test/}
\NormalTok{    ├── test\_copyright.py}
\NormalTok{    ├── test\_flake8.py}
\NormalTok{    └── test\_pep257.py}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The outer \texttt{my\_robot\_package/} is the ROS package root. The
inner directory is the Python import package used by
\texttt{import\ my\_robot\_package}; the repeated name does not indicate
nested repositories.

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
File or directory
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Purpose
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{package.xml} & Declares package identity, licence, dependencies,
and build type \\
\texttt{setup.py} & Selects Python packages, data files, and executables
to install \\
\texttt{setup.cfg} & Selects the ROS-compatible destination for Python
executables \\
\texttt{resource/my\_robot\_package} & Registers the installed package
in the ament resource index \\
\texttt{my\_robot\_package/} & Contains importable Python source \\
\texttt{test/} & Contains checks run by \texttt{pytest} and
\texttt{colcon\ test} \\
\end{longtable}
}

The resource marker is intentionally empty. Its filename identifies the
package.

\section{Complete the package
metadata}\label{complete-the-package-metadata}

Replace placeholder descriptions and maintainer details in
\texttt{package.xml} and \texttt{setup.py}. Keep their package name,
version, and licence consistent. The \texttt{package.xml} export tells
\texttt{colcon} which build integration to use:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{\textless{}}\KeywordTok{export}\NormalTok{\textgreater{}}
\NormalTok{  \textless{}}\KeywordTok{build\_type}\NormalTok{\textgreater{}ament\_python\textless{}/}\KeywordTok{build\_type}\NormalTok{\textgreater{}}
\NormalTok{\textless{}/}\KeywordTok{export}\NormalTok{\textgreater{}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

A concise \texttt{setup.py} for the example is:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ setuptools }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ find\_packages, setup}

\NormalTok{package\_name }\OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{"my\_robot\_package"}

\NormalTok{setup(}
\NormalTok{    name}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{package\_name,}
\NormalTok{    version}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"0.0.0"}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    packages}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{find\_packages(exclude}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[}\StringTok{"test"}\NormalTok{]),}
\NormalTok{    data\_files}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[}
\NormalTok{        (}
            \StringTok{"share/ament\_index/resource\_index/packages"}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{            [}\SpecialStringTok{f"resource/}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{package\_name}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{],}
\NormalTok{        ),}
\NormalTok{        (}\SpecialStringTok{f"share/}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{package\_name}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{, [}\StringTok{"package.xml"}\NormalTok{]),}
\NormalTok{    ],}
\NormalTok{    install\_requires}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[}\StringTok{"setuptools"}\NormalTok{],}
\NormalTok{    zip\_safe}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    maintainer}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"Your Name"}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    maintainer\_email}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"you@example.com"}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    description}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"Example ROS 2 Python package."}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    license}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"Apache{-}2.0"}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    entry\_points}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}
        \StringTok{"console\_scripts"}\NormalTok{: [}
            \StringTok{"my\_node = my\_robot\_package.my\_node:main"}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        ],}
\NormalTok{    \},}
\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The important connections are:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{packages} installs importable Python packages.
\item
  \texttt{data\_files} installs the resource marker and
  \texttt{package.xml} where ROS expects them.
\item
  \texttt{console\_scripts} creates an executable that calls a Python
  function.
\end{itemize}

The entry point \texttt{my\_node\ =\ my\_robot\_package.my\_node:main}
maps the executable name \texttt{my\_node} to the function
\texttt{main()} in the Python module
\texttt{my\_robot\_package.my\_node}.

The matching \texttt{setup.cfg} is:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{[develop]}
\DataTypeTok{script\_dir}\OtherTok{=}\StringTok{$base/lib/my\_robot\_package}

\KeywordTok{[install]}
\DataTypeTok{install\_scripts}\OtherTok{=}\StringTok{$base/lib/my\_robot\_package}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Here, \texttt{\$base} is the package installation prefix.
\texttt{setup.cfg} specifies that executable scripts belong under
\texttt{lib/my\_robot\_package}, where \texttt{ros2\ run} looks for
them. It does not create an executable by itself; the
\texttt{console\_scripts} entry does that.

\section{Resolve dependencies and run fast
tests}\label{resolve-dependencies-and-run-fast-tests}

Resolve dependencies declared by packages under \texttt{src/}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ExtensionTok{rosdep}\NormalTok{ install }\DataTypeTok{\textbackslash{}}
  \AttributeTok{{-}{-}from{-}paths}\NormalTok{ src }\DataTypeTok{\textbackslash{}}
  \AttributeTok{{-}{-}ignore{-}src} \DataTypeTok{\textbackslash{}}
  \AttributeTok{{-}{-}rosdistro}\NormalTok{ jazzy }\DataTypeTok{\textbackslash{}}
  \AttributeTok{{-}r} \DataTypeTok{\textbackslash{}}
  \AttributeTok{{-}y}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{rosdep} ignores dependencies supplied by source packages in the
workspace, maps the remaining dependency keys to operating-system
packages, and installs missing packages.

Run fast tests directly against the source tree:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ExtensionTok{python3} \AttributeTok{{-}m}\NormalTok{ pytest }\DataTypeTok{\textbackslash{}}
\NormalTok{  src/my\_robot\_package/test }\AttributeTok{{-}q}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Source-level tests provide fast feedback but do not verify ROS metadata
or the installed layout. Continue only when they pass; the count and
duration depend on the package.

\section{Build and install the
package}\label{build-and-install-the-package}

From the workspace root, run:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ExtensionTok{colcon}\NormalTok{ build }\DataTypeTok{\textbackslash{}}
  \AttributeTok{{-}{-}packages{-}select}\NormalTok{ my\_robot\_package }\DataTypeTok{\textbackslash{}}
  \AttributeTok{{-}{-}symlink{-}install}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\textbf{What happens:} \texttt{colcon} discovers packages under
\texttt{src/}, reads their manifests, orders them by declared
dependencies, and selects the appropriate build integration.
\texttt{ament\_python} then invokes \texttt{setuptools} using
\texttt{setup.py} and \texttt{setup.cfg}.

The logical installed layout is:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{install/my\_robot\_package/}
\NormalTok{├── lib/}
\NormalTok{│   ├── python3.12/}
\NormalTok{│   │   └── site{-}packages/}
\NormalTok{│   │       └── my\_robot\_package/}
\NormalTok{│   │           ├── \_\_init\_\_.py}
\NormalTok{│   │           └── my\_node.py}
\NormalTok{│   └── my\_robot\_package/}
\NormalTok{│       └── my\_node}
\NormalTok{└── share/}
\NormalTok{    ├── ament\_index/}
\NormalTok{    │   └── resource\_index/}
\NormalTok{    │       └── packages/}
\NormalTok{    │           └── my\_robot\_package}
\NormalTok{    └── my\_robot\_package/}
\NormalTok{        └── package.xml}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The Python version in the path depends on the system.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{site-packages/my\_robot\_package} supplies importable Python
  code.
\item
  \texttt{lib/my\_robot\_package/my\_node} supplies the executable used
  by \texttt{ros2\ run}.
\item
  The resource marker registers the package with ROS.
\item
  The installed \texttt{package.xml} supplies package metadata.
\end{itemize}

ROS 2 needs this standard runtime layout to locate packages,
executables, metadata, launch files, configuration, Python imports, and
dependency environment hooks.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{What \texttt{-\/-symlink-install}
changes}{What -\/-symlink-install changes}}\label{what---symlink-install-changes}

Supported files are linked back to editable source rather than copied:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{install/.../my\_node.py}
\NormalTok{        ↓ symbolic link}
\NormalTok{src/my\_robot\_package/my\_robot\_package/my\_node.py}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Python packages may use an \texttt{.egg-link} instead of the copied
directory shown in the logical tree. Consequently, edits to existing
linked Python files may appear without rebuilding, but \texttt{install/}
is still needed for metadata, executables, and environment scripts.

Rebuild after changing \texttt{setup.py}, \texttt{setup.cfg},
\texttt{console\_scripts}, installed data, dependencies, CMake
configuration, or compiled C++ code. Rebuild after structural changes
and before formal verification to avoid stale state.

\section{Source the overlay and run the
package}\label{source-the-overlay-and-run-the-package}

After a successful build, source the script matching the current shell.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\BuiltInTok{source}\NormalTok{ install/setup.zsh}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\BuiltInTok{source}\NormalTok{ install/setup.bash}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This command does not install anything. It adds the workspace's
\texttt{install/} tree as an \textbf{overlay} on the ROS underlay by
updating variables such as \texttt{AMENT\_PREFIX\_PATH},
\texttt{PYTHONPATH}, and \texttt{PATH}.

Sourcing affects only the current shell. A new terminal must source the
ROS underlay and then the workspace overlay again.

Verify package discovery, Python import, and executable discovery:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ExtensionTok{ros2}\NormalTok{ pkg prefix my\_robot\_package}
\ExtensionTok{python3} \AttributeTok{{-}c} \StringTok{"import my\_robot\_package"}
\ExtensionTok{ros2}\NormalTok{ run my\_robot\_package my\_node}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The final command should print:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Hi from my\_robot\_package.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{ros2\ pkg\ prefix} queries the ament resource index.
\texttt{ros2\ run} uses the first argument as the package name and the
second as the executable name, then starts
\texttt{lib/my\_robot\_package/my\_node}.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Run tests through
\texttt{colcon}}{Run tests through colcon}}\label{run-tests-through-colcon}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ExtensionTok{colcon}\NormalTok{ test }\AttributeTok{{-}{-}packages{-}select}\NormalTok{ my\_robot\_package}
\ExtensionTok{colcon}\NormalTok{ test{-}result }\AttributeTok{{-}{-}verbose}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{colcon\ test} runs the package's configured tests and stores
structured results under the build tree. \texttt{colcon\ test-result}
aggregates them. A completed command is not necessarily successful;
verification passes only when the summary reports no errors or failures.

\section{Repeat the daily development
loop}\label{repeat-the-daily-development-loop}

From the workspace root, repeat:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# 1. Edit source.}
\ExtensionTok{vim}\NormalTok{ src/my\_robot\_package/my\_robot\_package/my\_node.py}

\CommentTok{\# 2. Run fast tests.}
\ExtensionTok{python3} \AttributeTok{{-}m}\NormalTok{ pytest src/my\_robot\_package/test }\AttributeTok{{-}q}

\CommentTok{\# 3. Build and install.}
\ExtensionTok{colcon}\NormalTok{ build }\DataTypeTok{\textbackslash{}}
  \AttributeTok{{-}{-}packages{-}select}\NormalTok{ my\_robot\_package }\DataTypeTok{\textbackslash{}}
  \AttributeTok{{-}{-}symlink{-}install}

\CommentTok{\# 4. Reload the overlay.}
\BuiltInTok{source}\NormalTok{ install/setup.zsh}

\CommentTok{\# 5. Run the executable.}
\ExtensionTok{ros2}\NormalTok{ run my\_robot\_package my\_node}

\CommentTok{\# 6. Run package{-}level verification.}
\ExtensionTok{colcon}\NormalTok{ test }\AttributeTok{{-}{-}packages{-}select}\NormalTok{ my\_robot\_package}
\ExtensionTok{colcon}\NormalTok{ test{-}result }\AttributeTok{{-}{-}verbose}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Use \texttt{source\ install/setup.bash} in Bash.

\section*{Common failure checks}\label{common-failure-checks}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Common failure checks}

\markright{Common failure checks}

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Symptom
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Check
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{ros2:\ command\ not\ found} & Source the ROS underlay in the
current shell \\
Package absent from \texttt{colcon\ list} & Check its location under
\texttt{src/} and validate \texttt{package.xml} \\
Python package is not installed & Check \texttt{\_\_init\_\_.py} and the
\texttt{packages} argument in \texttt{setup.py} \\
\texttt{ros2\ pkg\ prefix} cannot find the package & Build and source
the overlay \\
\texttt{ros2\ run} cannot find the executable & Check
\texttt{console\_scripts} and \texttt{setup.cfg}, then rebuild and
source \\
Test status is unclear & Run
\texttt{colcon\ test-result\ -\/-verbose} \\
\end{longtable}
}

\chapter{\texorpdfstring{Planar Rigid-Body Motion: \(SE(2)\) and
Twists}{Planar Rigid-Body Motion: SE(2) and Twists}}\label{sec-planar-rigid-body-motion}

\section*{Why this chapter matters}\label{why-this-chapter-matters-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Why this chapter matters}

\markright{Why this chapter matters}

The differential-drive pose \(\mathbf q=[x\;y\;\theta]^\mathsf T\) is
convenient for storing three coordinates, but it does not by itself
expose how rigid motions compose, how the same motion is expressed in
different frames, or why constant body velocity produces the exact
curved-motion update derived previously. This chapter develops the
planar rigid-motion structure needed to answer those questions. It
begins by distinguishing a pose from its coordinates and representing a
planar pose by a homogeneous transformation in the special Euclidean
group \(SE(2)\).

\section*{Prerequisites and
assumptions}\label{prerequisites-and-assumptions-3}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Prerequisites and assumptions}

\markright{Prerequisites and assumptions}

The reader should understand matrices and matrix multiplication from
Chapter~\ref{sec-linear-algebra-foundations}, coordinate frames and
rotation matrices from Chapter~\ref{sec-geometry-coordinate-frames}, and
planar pose notation from Chapter~\ref{sec-differential-drive-model}.

All frames in this learning block are right-handed planar frames
embedded in three-dimensional physical space with a common vertical
axis. Frame \(\{w\}\) is the fixed world frame and frame \(\{b\}\) is
attached rigidly to the robot body. Distances are measured in metres and
angles in radians.

\section{Pose coordinates and rigid
transformations}\label{pose-coordinates-and-rigid-transformations}

\subsection{A pose is a relative geometric
relationship}\label{a-pose-is-a-relative-geometric-relationship}

A planar rigid body's \textbf{pose} specifies both its position and its
orientation relative to a reference frame. The word \emph{relative} is
essential: saying that a robot is at position \((2,1)\) with heading
\(\pi/2\) is incomplete until the reference frame and sign convention
are known.

Let \(O_w\) and \(O_b\) denote the origins of frames \(\{w\}\) and
\(\{b\}\). The position of \(O_b\) relative to \(O_w\), expressed in
world-frame coordinates, is

\[
{}^{w}\mathbf p_b
=
\begin{bmatrix}
x\\
y
\end{bmatrix}
\in\mathbb R^2,
\]

where \(x\) and \(y\) are measured in metres. The orientation of frame
\(\{b\}\) relative to frame \(\{w\}\) is represented by the rotation
matrix \(\mathbf R_{wb}(\theta)\in\mathbb R^{2\times2}\):

\[
\mathbf R_{wb}(\theta)
=
\begin{bmatrix}
\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\
\sin\theta&\cos\theta
\end{bmatrix}.
\]

The subscript order \(wb\) means that \(\mathbf R_{wb}\) maps vector
coordinates from frame \(\{b\}\) to frame \(\{w\}\). The pose of
\(\{b\}\) relative to \(\{w\}\) can therefore be described by the pair

\[
\left(\mathbf R_{wb},{}^{w}\mathbf p_b\right).
\]

This pair separates orientation from position. A homogeneous
transformation will combine them into one matrix without confusing their
different roles.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Planar rotations and
\(SO(2)\)}{Planar rotations and SO(2)}}\label{sec-se2-planar-rotations}

The set of valid planar rotation matrices is called the \textbf{special
orthogonal group in two dimensions}, written \(SO(2)\) and defined by

\[
SO(2)
\mathrel{:=}
\left\{
\mathbf R\in\mathbb R^{2\times2}
\;\middle|\;
\mathbf R^\mathsf T\mathbf R=\mathbf I_2,
\quad
\det(\mathbf R)=1
\right\}.
\]

The word \emph{orthogonal} refers to
\(\mathbf R^\mathsf T\mathbf R=\mathbf I_2\), which means that the
columns form an orthonormal basis and that
\(\mathbf R^{-1}=\mathbf R^\mathsf T\). The word \emph{special} adds the
condition \(\det(\mathbf R)=1\), excluding reflections whose determinant
is \(-1\).

The word \emph{group} means that the set is equipped with an operation
satisfying four properties: closure, associativity, an identity element,
and an inverse for every element. For \(SO(2)\), the operation is matrix
multiplication.

\textbf{Closure} means that multiplying any two elements of \(SO(2)\)
produces another element of \(SO(2)\). If \(\mathbf R(\alpha)\) and
\(\mathbf R(\beta)\) represent planar rotations through angles
\(\alpha\) and \(\beta\), then

\[
\mathbf R(\alpha)\mathbf R(\beta)
=
\mathbf R(\alpha+\beta)
\in SO(2).
\]

Geometrically, performing one planar rotation and then another still
produces a valid planar rotation; it does not introduce scaling, shear,
or reflection.

\textbf{Associativity} means that the placement of parentheses does not
change the result of composing three rotations:

\[
\left(\mathbf R_1\mathbf R_2\right)\mathbf R_3
=
\mathbf R_1\left(\mathbf R_2\mathbf R_3\right).
\]

Associativity changes only the grouping, not the order, of the matrices.
It should not be confused with commutativity, which would permit the
order to be exchanged. The identity element is \(\mathbf I_2\), and
every \(\mathbf R\in SO(2)\) has the inverse
\(\mathbf R^\mathsf T\in SO(2)\).

Every planar rotation matrix can be written as \(\mathbf R(\theta)\) for
some \(\theta\in\mathbb R\). The angle is not unique because

\[
\mathbf R(\theta+2\pi n)=\mathbf R(\theta),
\qquad n\in\mathbb Z,
\]

where \(\mathbb Z\) is the set of integers. Thus, \(\theta\) is a
\textbf{coordinate} used to describe an orientation, while
\(\mathbf R(\theta)\) represents the \textbf{orientation} itself. Angles
that differ by an integer multiple of \(2\pi\) describe the same element
of \(SO(2)\).

\subsection{Transforming a point by rotation and
translation}\label{sec-se2-transforming-point}

Let \(P\) be a geometric point fixed to the robot. Its position relative
to \(O_b\), expressed in body-frame coordinates, is
\({}^{b}\mathbf p_P\in\mathbb R^2\) and is measured in metres. Its
world-frame position is obtained by first rotating its body-frame
displacement and then adding the world-frame position of the body
origin:

\[
\boxed{
{}^{w}\mathbf p_P
=
\mathbf R_{wb}\,{}^{b}\mathbf p_P
+
{}^{w}\mathbf p_b
}.
\]

This equation follows by tracing the geometric displacement from \(O_w\)
to \(P\). First travel from \(O_w\) to \(O_b\) by \({}^{w}\mathbf p_b\).
Then travel from \(O_b\) to \(P\) by \({}^{b}\mathbf p_P\), but rotate
that second displacement with \(\mathbf R_{wb}\) so that both terms are
expressed in world-frame coordinates before adding them.

A free vector, such as a displacement or velocity vector, has direction
and magnitude but no fixed origin. It therefore changes coordinates
using rotation alone:

\[
{}^{w}\mathbf v
=
\mathbf R_{wb}\,{}^{b}\mathbf v.
\]

Translation appears in the point equation because a point is located
relative to a frame origin. It does not appear in the free-vector
equation because a free vector is not attached to an origin.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Homogeneous transformations and
\(SE(2)\)}{Homogeneous transformations and SE(2)}}\label{homogeneous-transformations-and-se2}

The point mapping in Section~\ref{sec-se2-transforming-point} contains
both a matrix multiplication and a vector addition. Homogeneous
coordinates package these two operations into one matrix multiplication.
Define the homogeneous coordinate column of point \(P\) by appending a
\(1\):

\[
{}^{b}\overline{\mathbf p}_P
\mathrel{:=}
\begin{bmatrix}
{}^{b}\mathbf p_P\\
1
\end{bmatrix}
\in\mathbb R^3.
\]

The overline identifies a homogeneous coordinate representation, not a
different geometric point. Define the \textbf{homogeneous
transformation} from frame \(\{b\}\) to frame \(\{w\}\) by

\[
\boxed{
\mathbf T_{wb}
\mathrel{:=}
\begin{bmatrix}
\mathbf R_{wb}&{}^{w}\mathbf p_b\\
\mathbf 0_2^\mathsf T&1
\end{bmatrix}
}
\in\mathbb R^{3\times3},
\]

where \(\mathbf 0_2=[0\;0]^\mathsf T\in\mathbb R^2\). The subscript
order means that \(\mathbf T_{wb}\) maps homogeneous point coordinates
from frame \(\{b\}\) to frame \(\{w\}\):

\[
\begin{aligned}
{}^{w}\overline{\mathbf p}_P
&=
\mathbf T_{wb}\,{}^{b}\overline{\mathbf p}_P\\
&=
\begin{bmatrix}
\mathbf R_{wb}&{}^{w}\mathbf p_b\\
\mathbf 0_2^\mathsf T&1
\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}
{}^{b}\mathbf p_P\\
1
\end{bmatrix}\\
&=
\begin{bmatrix}
\mathbf R_{wb}\,{}^{b}\mathbf p_P+{}^{w}\mathbf p_b\\
1
\end{bmatrix}.
\end{aligned}
\]

The upper two entries reproduce the point-transformation equation.
Appending \(1\) causes the translation column to contribute to the
result. Thus, \(\mathbf T_{wb}\) rotates the point's body-frame
displacement into the world frame and then adds the world position of
the body origin.

Using \({}^{w}\mathbf p_b=[x\;y]^\mathsf T\) and
\(\mathbf R_{wb}=\mathbf R_{wb}(\theta)\) gives the component form

\[
\mathbf T_{wb}
=
\begin{bmatrix}
\cos\theta&-\sin\theta&x\\
\sin\theta&\cos\theta&y\\
0&0&1
\end{bmatrix}.
\]

The rotation block is dimensionless, while the first two entries of the
translation column have units of metres. The final row is an algebraic
device that permits rotation and translation to be applied by one matrix
multiplication; it does not represent another physical spatial
dimension.

To preserve the rotation-only mapping for a free vector, define its
homogeneous representation by appending a \(0\):

\[
{}^{b}\overline{\mathbf v}
\mathrel{:=}
\begin{bmatrix}
{}^{b}\mathbf v\\
0
\end{bmatrix}.
\]

Then

\[
\mathbf T_{wb}
\begin{bmatrix}
{}^{b}\mathbf v\\
0
\end{bmatrix}
=
\begin{bmatrix}
\mathbf R_{wb}\,{}^{b}\mathbf v\\
0
\end{bmatrix}.
\]

Appending \(0\) prevents the translation column from contributing. This
algebraic distinction encodes the geometric fact that changing a point's
reference frame requires rotation and translation, whereas changing the
coordinates of a free vector requires only rotation.

The set of all planar homogeneous transformations is the \textbf{special
Euclidean group in two dimensions}:

\[
SE(2)
\mathrel{:=}
\left\{
\begin{bmatrix}
\mathbf R&\mathbf p\\
\mathbf 0_2^\mathsf T&1
\end{bmatrix}
\;\middle|\;
\mathbf R\in SO(2),
\quad
\mathbf p\in\mathbb R^2
\right\}.
\]

Here, \emph{Euclidean} indicates rigid motions that preserve Euclidean
distances and angles. Matrix multiplication is the group operation, the
\(3\times3\) identity \(\mathbf I_3\) is the identity motion, and every
element has an inverse that reverses the coordinate mapping.
Section~\ref{sec-se2-inverting-transformation} derives that inverse
explicitly.

\subsection{Composing transformations}\label{composing-transformations}

Introduce a third frame \(\{c\}\). Let \(\mathbf T_{ab}\in SE(2)\) map
coordinates from frame \(\{b\}\) to frame \(\{a\}\), and let
\(\mathbf T_{bc}\in SE(2)\) map coordinates from frame \(\{c\}\) to
frame \(\{b\}\). Applying the two mappings to a point gives

\[
{}^{a}\overline{\mathbf p}_P
=
\mathbf T_{ab}
\left(
\mathbf T_{bc}\,{}^{c}\overline{\mathbf p}_P
\right)
=
\left(
\mathbf T_{ab}\mathbf T_{bc}
\right)
{}^{c}\overline{\mathbf p}_P.
\]

Therefore, the transform that maps directly from frame \(\{c\}\) to
frame \(\{a\}\) is

\[
\boxed{
\mathbf T_{ac}
=
\mathbf T_{ab}\mathbf T_{bc}
}.
\]

The adjacent frame label \(b\) identifies the intermediate frame.
Expanding the block multiplication gives

\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbf T_{ab}\mathbf T_{bc}
&=
\begin{bmatrix}
\mathbf R_{ab}&{}^{a}\mathbf p_b\\
\mathbf 0_2^\mathsf T&1
\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}
\mathbf R_{bc}&{}^{b}\mathbf p_c\\
\mathbf 0_2^\mathsf T&1
\end{bmatrix}\\
&=
\begin{bmatrix}
\mathbf R_{ab}\mathbf R_{bc}&
\mathbf R_{ab}\,{}^{b}\mathbf p_c+{}^{a}\mathbf p_b\\
\mathbf 0_2^\mathsf T&1
\end{bmatrix}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Consequently,

\[
\boxed{
\mathbf R_{ac}=\mathbf R_{ab}\mathbf R_{bc},
\qquad
{}^{a}\mathbf p_c
=
\mathbf R_{ab}\,{}^{b}\mathbf p_c
+
{}^{a}\mathbf p_b
}.
\]

The translation vectors cannot be added directly until they are
expressed in the same frame. The product first rotates
\({}^{b}\mathbf p_c\) into frame \(\{a\}\) and only then adds
\({}^{a}\mathbf p_b\).

\subsection{Inverting a
transformation}\label{sec-se2-inverting-transformation}

The inverse transform \(\mathbf T_{ba}=\mathbf T_{ab}^{-1}\) must undo
both the translation and the rotation. Starting from the point mapping

\[
{}^{a}\mathbf p_P
=
\mathbf R_{ab}\,{}^{b}\mathbf p_P
+
{}^{a}\mathbf p_b,
\]

subtract \({}^{a}\mathbf p_b\) and premultiply by
\(\mathbf R_{ab}^{-1}=\mathbf R_{ab}^\mathsf T\):

\[
{}^{b}\mathbf p_P
=
\mathbf R_{ab}^\mathsf T
\left(
{}^{a}\mathbf p_P-{}^{a}\mathbf p_b
\right).
\]

Distributing the rotation gives

\[
{}^{b}\mathbf p_P
=
\mathbf R_{ab}^\mathsf T{}^{a}\mathbf p_P
-
\mathbf R_{ab}^\mathsf T{}^{a}\mathbf p_b.
\]

Therefore,

\[
\boxed{
\mathbf T_{ab}^{-1}
=
\mathbf T_{ba}
=
\begin{bmatrix}
\mathbf R_{ab}^\mathsf T&
-\mathbf R_{ab}^\mathsf T{}^{a}\mathbf p_b\\
\mathbf 0_2^\mathsf T&1
\end{bmatrix}
}.
\]

The inverse translation is not generally \(-{}^{a}\mathbf p_b\) because
that column is expressed in frame \(\{a\}\). It must first be negated
and then expressed in frame \(\{b\}\) by \(\mathbf R_{ab}^\mathsf T\).

\subsection{Pose coordinates versus the pose
itself}\label{pose-coordinates-versus-the-pose-itself}

The familiar column

\[
\mathbf q
=
\begin{bmatrix}
x\\y\\\theta
\end{bmatrix}
\]

has the following meaning:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(x\in\mathbb R\) is the \(x_w\)-coordinate of the body origin
  \(O_b\), expressed in the world frame and measured in metres;
\item
  \(y\in\mathbb R\) is the \(y_w\)-coordinate of \(O_b\), expressed in
  the world frame and measured in metres; and
\item
  \(\theta\in\mathbb R\) is the counter-clockwise orientation of body
  frame \(\{b\}\) relative to world frame \(\{w\}\), measured in
  radians.
\end{itemize}

Therefore, the position of the body origin relative to the world origin,
expressed in world-frame coordinates, is

\[
{}^w\mathbf p_b
=
\begin{bmatrix}
x\\y
\end{bmatrix}.
\]

The notation \({}^w\mathbf p_b\) means ``the position of the body origin
\(O_b\), expressed in world-frame coordinates.'' Thus, \(\mathbf q\) is
a coordinate description of the pose of frame \(\{b\}\) relative to
frame \(\{w\}\). The same pose can be represented by
\(\mathbf T_{wb}\in SE(2)\):

\[
\boxed{
\mathbf q
=
\begin{bmatrix}
x\\y\\\theta
\end{bmatrix}
\quad\longmapsto\quad
\mathbf T_{wb}(\mathbf q)
=
\begin{bmatrix}
\cos\theta&-\sin\theta&x\\
\sin\theta&\cos\theta&y\\
0&0&1
\end{bmatrix}
}.
\]

The coordinate column is useful for storage, plotting, and differential
equations, but it is not an ordinary geometric vector. Its components
represent different physical quantities, \(\theta\) and
\(\theta+2\pi n\) represent the same orientation, and componentwise
addition does not in general reproduce rigid-motion composition.
Transformations should be composed by matrix multiplication with
explicit frame labels.

\subsection{Worked example}\label{worked-example}

Suppose the body origin is at

\[
{}^{w}\mathbf p_b
=
\begin{bmatrix}
2\\1
\end{bmatrix}
\mathrm m
\]

and the body heading is \(\theta=\pi/2\,\mathrm{rad}\). A point \(P\)
fixed one metre along the body \(+x_b\)-axis has body-frame coordinates

\[
{}^{b}\mathbf p_P
=
\begin{bmatrix}
1\\0
\end{bmatrix}
\mathrm m.
\]

The rotation and homogeneous transformation are

\[
\mathbf R_{wb}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)
=
\begin{bmatrix}
0&-1\\
1&0
\end{bmatrix},
\qquad
\mathbf T_{wb}
=
\begin{bmatrix}
0&-1&2\\
1&0&1\\
0&0&1
\end{bmatrix}.
\]

Applying the transform gives

\[
\begin{aligned}
{}^{w}\overline{\mathbf p}_P
&=
\mathbf T_{wb}{}^{b}\overline{\mathbf p}_P\\
&=
\begin{bmatrix}
0&-1&2\\
1&0&1\\
0&0&1
\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}
1\\0\\1
\end{bmatrix}\\
&=
\begin{bmatrix}
2\\2\\1
\end{bmatrix}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus, \({}^{w}\mathbf p_P=[2\;2]^\mathsf T\,\mathrm m\). The one-metre
body-frame displacement is rotated into the world \(+y_w\)-direction and
then added to the body origin at \([2\;1]^\mathsf T\,\mathrm m\).

\section*{\texorpdfstring{Quick test A: pose and
\(SE(2)\)}{Quick test A: pose and SE(2)}}\label{quick-test-a-pose-and-se2}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quick test A: pose and \(SE(2)\)}

\markright{Quick test A: pose and \(SE(2)\)}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  What geometric information is contained in the pair
  \((\mathbf R_{wb},{}^{w}\mathbf p_b)\), and what does the subscript
  order \(wb\) mean?
\item
  Define \(SO(2)\). What do its orthogonality and determinant conditions
  exclude or guarantee?
\item
  Why do \(\theta\) and \(\theta+2\pi\) describe the same orientation?
\item
  Starting from the block definitions of \(\mathbf T_{ab}\) and
  \(\mathbf T_{bc}\), derive the translation column of
  \(\mathbf T_{ac}=\mathbf T_{ab}\mathbf T_{bc}\).
\item
  Why is the inverse translation
  \(-\mathbf R_{ab}^\mathsf T{}^{a}\mathbf p_b\) rather than simply
  \(-{}^{a}\mathbf p_b\)?
\item
  A body frame has world position \([3\;2]^\mathsf T\,\mathrm m\) and
  heading \(-\pi/2\,\mathrm{rad}\). Find the world coordinates of a
  point whose body coordinates are \([0.5\;0]^\mathsf T\,\mathrm m\).
\item
  Explain why \(\mathbf q_1+\mathbf q_2\) is not generally the
  composition of the two poses represented by \(\mathbf q_1\) and
  \(\mathbf q_2\).
\item
  Name two transformations that cannot be represented by an element of
  \(SE(2)\) and explain why.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection*{Answers and hints}\label{answers-and-hints-10}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Answers and hints}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \(\mathbf R_{wb}\) describes the orientation of frame \(\{b\}\)
  relative to frame \(\{w\}\), while \({}^{w}\mathbf p_b\) locates
  \(O_b\) relative to \(O_w\) using world coordinates. The order \(wb\)
  means that the rotation maps coordinate columns from \(\{b\}\) to
  \(\{w\}\).
\item
  \(SO(2)=\{\mathbf R\in\mathbb R^{2\times2}\mid\mathbf R^\mathsf T\mathbf R=\mathbf I_2,\det\mathbf R=1\}\).
  Orthogonality preserves lengths and angles and gives
  \(\mathbf R^{-1}=\mathbf R^\mathsf T\); determinant \(+1\) preserves
  orientation and excludes reflections.
\item
  Sine and cosine are \(2\pi\)-periodic, so every entry of
  \(\mathbf R(\theta+2\pi)\) equals the corresponding entry of
  \(\mathbf R(\theta)\).
\item
  Block multiplication gives
  \({}^{a}\mathbf p_c=\mathbf R_{ab}{}^{b}\mathbf p_c+{}^{a}\mathbf p_b\).
  The first term rotates the \(b\)-frame translation into frame
  \(\{a\}\) before the two translations are added.
\item
  The reversed displacement must be expressed in frame \(\{b\}\),
  whereas \({}^{a}\mathbf p_b\) is expressed in frame \(\{a\}\).
  Premultiplication by \(\mathbf R_{ab}^\mathsf T\) performs the
  necessary coordinate change.
\item
  \(\mathbf R_{wb}(-\pi/2)[0.5\;0]^\mathsf T=[0\;-0.5]^\mathsf T\,\mathrm m\),
  so \({}^{w}\mathbf p_P=[3\;1.5]^\mathsf T\,\mathrm m\).
\item
  Rigid-motion composition must account for the frame in which each
  translation is expressed and for the order of rotations and
  translations. Componentwise addition does neither, and angular
  coordinates are periodic.
\item
  Examples include nonuniform scaling, shear, and elastic deformation.
  They do not preserve every distance and angle, so they are not rigid
  motions and do not belong to \(SE(2)\).
\end{enumerate}

\section{Passive coordinate changes and active rigid
motions}\label{passive-coordinate-changes-and-active-rigid-motions}

The same numerical rotation or homogeneous-transformation matrix can
appear in different geometric questions. Correct interpretation requires
identifying what remains fixed, what changes, and the frame in which
every coordinate column is expressed.

\subsection{Passive coordinate change}\label{passive-coordinate-change}

A \textbf{passive coordinate change} keeps the geometric point, vector,
or rigid body unchanged and changes only the coordinate frame used to
describe it. The word \emph{passive} indicates that nothing physically
moves.

For example, let the geometric point \(P\) remain fixed while its
coordinates are changed from frame \(\{b\}\) to frame \(\{w\}\). The
passive point-coordinate transformation is

\[
{}^{w}\overline{\mathbf p}_P
=
\mathbf T_{wb}\,{}^{b}\overline{\mathbf p}_P.
\]

The two coordinate columns \({}^{b}\overline{\mathbf p}_P\) and
\({}^{w}\overline{\mathbf p}_P\) contain different numbers in general,
but both describe the same geometric point \(P\). The transform
\(\mathbf T_{wb}\) accounts for the position and orientation of frame
\(\{b\}\) relative to frame \(\{w\}\).

For a free vector \(\mathbf v\), translation is irrelevant and only the
rotation block is used:

\[
{}^{w}\mathbf v
=
\mathbf R_{wb}\,{}^{b}\mathbf v.
\]

Suppose frame \(\{b\}\) is rotated counter-clockwise by \(\pi/2\)
relative to frame \(\{w\}\) and the vector lies along \(+x_b\). Its
world-frame coordinates are

\[
\begin{aligned}
{}^{w}\mathbf v
&=
\mathbf R_{wb}{}^{b}\mathbf v\\
&=
\begin{bmatrix}
0&-1\\
1&0
\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}
1\\0
\end{bmatrix}\\
&=
\begin{bmatrix}
0\\1
\end{bmatrix}.
\end{aligned}
\]

The vector has not rotated physically. It is the same geometric vector
described once using the axes of \(\{b\}\) and once using the axes of
\(\{w\}\).

\subsection{Active rigid-body motion}\label{active-rigid-body-motion}

An \textbf{active transformation} keeps the reference frame fixed and
moves a geometric point, vector, or rigid body. The initial point \(P\)
and final point \(P'\) are different geometric points, but both
coordinate columns are expressed in the same frame.

Let \(\mathbf R_{\Delta}\in SO(2)\) be an applied planar rotation and
let \(\mathbf d_{\Delta}\in\mathbb R^2\) be an applied translation
expressed in world-frame coordinates and measured in metres. Define the
active rigid-motion operator

\[
\mathbf T_{\Delta}
\mathrel{:=}
\begin{bmatrix}
\mathbf R_{\Delta}&\mathbf d_{\Delta}\\
\mathbf 0_2^\mathsf T&1
\end{bmatrix}
\in SE(2).
\]

Applied to point \(P\), it produces the new point \(P'\) according to

\[
\boxed{
{}^{w}\overline{\mathbf p}_{P'}
=
\mathbf T_{\Delta}\,{}^{w}\overline{\mathbf p}_{P}
},
\]

or, using ordinary two-dimensional coordinates,

\[
{}^{w}\mathbf p_{P'}
=
\mathbf R_{\Delta}{}^{w}\mathbf p_P
+
\mathbf d_{\Delta}.
\]

Both sides are expressed in frame \(\{w\}\). The point changes from
\(P\) to \(P'\), unlike a passive coordinate change, where the point
remains \(P\) and the leading frame superscript changes.

For example, let \(\mathbf R_{\Delta}=\mathbf R(\pi/2)\),
\(\mathbf d_{\Delta}=[2\;1]^\mathsf T\,\mathrm m\), and
\({}^{w}\mathbf p_P=[1\;0]^\mathsf T\,\mathrm m\). Then

\[
{}^{w}\mathbf p_{P'}
=
\begin{bmatrix}
0&-1\\
1&0
\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}
1\\0
\end{bmatrix}
+
\begin{bmatrix}
2\\1
\end{bmatrix}
=
\begin{bmatrix}
2\\2
\end{bmatrix}
\mathrm m.
\]

Here, the coordinates changed because the geometric point moved, not
because a different coordinate frame was selected.

\subsection{One matrix form, three
interpretations}\label{one-matrix-form-three-interpretations}

A homogeneous transformation may describe a relative pose, perform a
passive coordinate change, or act as an active rigid-motion operator.
The matrix arithmetic can be identical while the geometric meaning
differs.

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Interpretation
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
What changes?
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Representative statement
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Relative-pose description & Nothing is being operated on; the matrix
records the pose of one frame relative to another. & \(\mathbf T_{wb}\)
describes frame \(\{b\}\) relative to frame \(\{w\}\). \\
Passive coordinate change & The coordinate column and its frame label
change; the geometric point remains fixed. &
\({}^{w}\overline{\mathbf p}_P=\mathbf T_{wb}{}^{b}\overline{\mathbf p}_P\). \\
Active rigid motion & The geometric point changes; the coordinate frame
remains fixed. &
\({}^{w}\overline{\mathbf p}_{P'}=\mathbf T_{\Delta}{}^{w}\overline{\mathbf p}_P\). \\
\end{longtable}
}

The project notation reserves labelled transforms such as
\(\mathbf T_{wb}\) for relative poses and coordinate mappings. A symbol
such as \(\mathbf T_{\Delta}\) must be explicitly declared when the same
matrix form is used as an active motion operator.

\subsection{Composition order}\label{composition-order}

Transformations act on column vectors from the left, so the rightmost
matrix acts first. For a passive coordinate chain,

\[
{}^{a}\overline{\mathbf p}_P
=
\mathbf T_{ab}\mathbf T_{bc}{}^{c}\overline{\mathbf p}_P,
\]

the rightmost transform \(\mathbf T_{bc}\) first changes coordinates
from frame \(\{c\}\) to frame \(\{b\}\), and \(\mathbf T_{ab}\) then
changes them from frame \(\{b\}\) to frame \(\{a\}\). Thus,

\[
\mathbf T_{ac}
=
\mathbf T_{ab}\mathbf T_{bc}.
\]

For two active motions \(\mathbf T_1\) and \(\mathbf T_2\),

\[
{}^{w}\overline{\mathbf p}_{P''}
=
\mathbf T_2\mathbf T_1{}^{w}\overline{\mathbf p}_{P},
\]

motion \(\mathbf T_1\) occurs first and motion \(\mathbf T_2\) occurs
second. Associativity permits the parentheses in a longer product to be
regrouped, but it does not permit the matrix order to be reversed.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Why composition in \(SE(2)\) is not
commutative}{Why composition in SE(2) is not commutative}}\label{sec-se2-noncommutative}

Planar rotations alone commute, but planar rigid transformations
generally do not because rotation changes the direction of a
translation. Consider a translation of one metre along \(+x_w\) and a
counter-clockwise rotation of \(\pi/2\) about \(O_w\):

\[
\mathbf T_D
=
\begin{bmatrix}
1&0&1\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1
\end{bmatrix},
\qquad
\mathbf T_R
=
\begin{bmatrix}
0&-1&0\\
1&0&0\\
0&0&1
\end{bmatrix}.
\]

Translate first and rotate second:

\[
\mathbf T_R\mathbf T_D
=
\begin{bmatrix}
0&-1&0\\
1&0&1\\
0&0&1
\end{bmatrix}.
\]

Rotate first and translate second:

\[
\mathbf T_D\mathbf T_R
=
\begin{bmatrix}
0&-1&1\\
1&0&0\\
0&0&1
\end{bmatrix}.
\]

The products differ. Applied to the world origin
\({}^{w}\overline{\mathbf p}_O=[0\;0\;1]^\mathsf T\), they give

\[
\mathbf T_R\mathbf T_D{}^{w}\overline{\mathbf p}_O
=
\begin{bmatrix}
0\\1\\1
\end{bmatrix},
\qquad
\mathbf T_D\mathbf T_R{}^{w}\overline{\mathbf p}_O
=
\begin{bmatrix}
1\\0\\1
\end{bmatrix}.
\]

Therefore,

\[
\boxed{
\mathbf T_R\mathbf T_D
\neq
\mathbf T_D\mathbf T_R
}.
\]

This noncommutativity is why a transformation sequence must state both
the individual motions and their order.

\subsection{Interpretation failures and engineering
checks}\label{interpretation-failures-and-engineering-checks}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Missing frame labels:} An equation such as
  \(\mathbf p'=\mathbf T\mathbf p\) does not reveal whether
  \(\mathbf T\) changes coordinates or moves the point. Declare the
  interpretation and frames.
\item
  \textbf{Applying transformations in the wrong order:} For
  column-vector notation, transformations act from right to left. In
  \(\mathbf T_2\mathbf T_1\mathbf p\), \(\mathbf T_1\) acts first and
  \(\mathbf T_2\) acts second. Verify an implementation using a point
  whose expected final location is easy to calculate.
\item
  \textbf{Mixing coordinate frames:} Translation columns may be added
  only after they are expressed in the same frame.
\item
  \textbf{Assuming wrong commutativity:} Reordering a rotation and
  translation generally changes the resulting pose.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Quick test B: interpretation and
composition}\label{quick-test-b-interpretation-and-composition}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quick test B: interpretation and
composition}

\markright{Quick test B: interpretation and composition}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Define a passive coordinate change. What changes, and what remains
  fixed?
\item
  Define an active rigid-body motion. What changes, and what remains
  fixed?
\item
  Explain the three interpretations of a homogeneous transformation used
  in this chapter.
\item
  In \(\mathbf T_{ab}\mathbf T_{bc}{}^{c}\overline{\mathbf p}_P\), which
  matrix acts first, and what frame sequence does the product represent?
\item
  For active motions, what sequence is represented by
  \(\mathbf T_2\mathbf T_1{}^{w}\overline{\mathbf p}_P\)?
\item
  Using \(\mathbf T_D\) and \(\mathbf T_R\) from
  Section~\ref{sec-se2-noncommutative}, explain geometrically why
  applying them to the origin in opposite orders gives different
  results.
\item
  Does the commutativity of planar rotations imply that all elements of
  \(SE(2)\) commute? Explain.
\item
  A program receives an unlabelled matrix \(\mathbf T\) and an
  unlabelled coordinate column \(\mathbf p\). What information is
  missing before the multiplication can be interpreted safely?
\end{enumerate}

\subsection*{Answers and hints}\label{answers-and-hints-11}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Answers and hints}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  A passive coordinate change preserves the geometric object and changes
  only the frame used to express its coordinates. The coordinate values
  and frame label may change.
\item
  An active rigid-body motion preserves the reference frame and moves
  the geometric object. The initial and final coordinates describe
  different points or poses in the same frame.
\item
  A homogeneous transformation can describe the pose of one frame
  relative to another, map coordinates of an unchanged object between
  frames, or actively move an object while its coordinates remain
  expressed in one frame.
\item
  \(\mathbf T_{bc}\) acts first, mapping from \(\{c\}\) to \(\{b\}\);
  \(\mathbf T_{ab}\) then maps from \(\{b\}\) to \(\{a\}\). The complete
  product maps from \(\{c\}\) to \(\{a\}\).
\item
  \(\mathbf T_1\) moves the point first, and \(\mathbf T_2\) moves the
  resulting point second.
\item
  Translating the origin first places it at \([1\;0]^\mathsf T\), which
  the subsequent rotation moves to \([0\;1]^\mathsf T\). Rotating the
  origin first leaves it at the origin, after which translation places
  it at \([1\;0]^\mathsf T\).
\item
  No.~Although \(SO(2)\) rotations commute with each other, an \(SE(2)\)
  transformation also contains translation. Rotation changes the
  direction of a translation, so general rotation-translation products
  do not commute.
\item
  The program needs to know whether \(\mathbf p\) represents a point or
  free vector, the frame in which it is expressed, the source and
  destination frames or active-motion interpretation of \(\mathbf T\),
  the units, and the intended multiplication order.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Time-varying rotations and rigid-body point
velocity}\label{time-varying-rotations-and-rigid-body-point-velocity}

A pose describes where a rigid body is at one instant. A time-varying
pose describes motion. This section differentiates the position and
orientation components of \(\mathbf T_{wb}(t)\) and derives the velocity
of any point fixed to the body.

\subsection{Translational and angular
velocity}\label{translational-and-angular-velocity}

Let the planar body pose vary continuously with time:

\[
\mathbf T_{wb}(t)
=
\begin{bmatrix}
\mathbf R_{wb}(t)&{}^{w}\mathbf p_b(t)\\
\mathbf 0_2^\mathsf T&1
\end{bmatrix}
\in SE(2),
\]

where \(t\in\mathbb R_{\geq0}\) is time in seconds. The world-frame
position of the body origin is

\[
{}^{w}\mathbf p_b(t)
=
\begin{bmatrix}
x(t)\\
y(t)
\end{bmatrix}
\in\mathbb R^2.
\]

Its derivative, taken with respect to the fixed world frame and
expressed in world-frame coordinates, is the translational velocity of
\(O_b\):

\[
\boxed{
{}^{w}\mathbf v_b(t)
\mathrel{:=}
\frac{d}{dt}{}^{w}\mathbf p_b(t)
=
\begin{bmatrix}
\dot x(t)\\
\dot y(t)
\end{bmatrix}
}
\in\mathbb R^2.
\]

The entries of \({}^{w}\mathbf v_b\) are measured in
\(\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\). The heading is \(\theta(t)\in\mathbb R\),
measured in radians, and its derivative is the signed yaw rate

\[
\boxed{
\omega(t)
\mathrel{:=}
\dot\theta(t)
}
\in\mathbb R,
\]

measured in \(\mathrm{rad\,s^{-1}}\). Positive \(\omega\) denotes
counter-clockwise rotation about the common \(+z\)-axis of the planar
frames.

\subsection{Deriving the rotation-matrix
derivative}\label{sec-se2-rotation-matrix-derivative}

The time-varying planar rotation matrix is

\[
\mathbf R_{wb}(t)
=
\begin{bmatrix}
\cos\theta(t)&-\sin\theta(t)\\
\sin\theta(t)&\cos\theta(t)
\end{bmatrix}.
\]

Differentiate every entry and apply the chain rule:

\[
\begin{aligned}
\dot{\mathbf R}_{wb}(t)
&=
\dot\theta(t)
\begin{bmatrix}
-\sin\theta(t)&-\cos\theta(t)\\
\cos\theta(t)&-\sin\theta(t)
\end{bmatrix}\\
&=
\omega(t)
\begin{bmatrix}
-\sin\theta(t)&-\cos\theta(t)\\
\cos\theta(t)&-\sin\theta(t)
\end{bmatrix}.
\end{aligned}
\]

A square matrix \(\mathbf A\) is \textbf{skew-symmetric} when its
transpose equals its negative:

\[
\boxed{
\mathbf A^\mathsf T
=
-\mathbf A
}.
\]

Transposition reflects the entries across the main diagonal. The
condition therefore means that entries mirrored across the diagonal have
equal magnitudes and opposite signs; every diagonal entry must be zero.
Define the dimensionless planar skew-symmetric generator

\[
\boxed{
\mathbf S_2
\mathrel{:=}
\begin{bmatrix}
0&-1\\
1&0
\end{bmatrix}
}.
\]

The matrix \(\mathbf S_2\) is skew-symmetric because
\(\mathbf S_2^\mathsf T=-\mathbf S_2\). Premultiplication by
\(\mathbf S_2\) rotates any planar coordinate column counter-clockwise
by \(\pi/2\):

\[
\mathbf S_2
\begin{bmatrix}
a\\b
\end{bmatrix}
=
\begin{bmatrix}
-b\\a
\end{bmatrix}.
\]

Direct multiplication gives

\[
\mathbf S_2\mathbf R_{wb}(\theta)
=
\mathbf R_{wb}(\theta)\mathbf S_2
=
\begin{bmatrix}
-\sin\theta&-\cos\theta\\
\cos\theta&-\sin\theta
\end{bmatrix}.
\]

Therefore,

\[
\boxed{
\dot{\mathbf R}_{wb}
=
\omega\mathbf S_2\mathbf R_{wb}
=
\mathbf R_{wb}\omega\mathbf S_2
}.
\]

The two products are equal in the planar case because every element of
\(SO(2)\) commutes with \(\mathbf S_2\). This equality is a special
simplification of planar rotation; matrix order becomes important for
three-dimensional angular velocity.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Why \(\dot{\mathbf R}_{wb}\) is not a
rotation
matrix}{Why \textbackslash dot\{\textbackslash mathbf R\}\_\{wb\} is not a rotation matrix}}\label{sec-se2-rotation-derivative-tangent}

Although \(\mathbf R_{wb}(t)\in SO(2)\) at every time \(t\), its
derivative \(\dot{\mathbf R}_{wb}(t)\) is generally not a rotation
matrix. A rotation matrix is a dimensionless map that represents a
finite change of orientation, whereas \(\dot{\mathbf R}_{wb}\) measures
how rapidly that map is changing and has entries with units of
\(\mathrm{s^{-1}}\). The derivative therefore describes an instantaneous
direction of rotational motion rather than another finite rotation.

\subsubsection{The rotation constraint restricts its
derivative}\label{the-rotation-constraint-restricts-its-derivative}

Every planar rotation matrix satisfies

\[
\mathbf R_{wb}^\mathsf T\mathbf R_{wb}
=
\mathbf I_2.
\]

Differentiating this constraint with respect to time gives

\[
\dot{\mathbf R}_{wb}^\mathsf T\mathbf R_{wb}
+
\mathbf R_{wb}^\mathsf T\dot{\mathbf R}_{wb}
=
\mathbf 0_{2\times2}.
\]

Rearranging shows that

\[
\left(
\mathbf R_{wb}^\mathsf T\dot{\mathbf R}_{wb}
\right)^\mathsf T
=
-\mathbf R_{wb}^\mathsf T\dot{\mathbf R}_{wb}.
\]

Thus, \(\mathbf R_{wb}^\mathsf T\dot{\mathbf R}_{wb}\) is
\textbf{skew-symmetric}: its transpose equals its negative. The factor
\(\mathbf R_{wb}^\mathsf T\) expresses the rotational rate relative to
the current body orientation; it does not turn \(\dot{\mathbf R}_{wb}\)
into a finite rotation.

The set of all \(2\times2\) real skew-symmetric matrices is

\[
\mathfrak{so}(2)
\mathrel{:=}
\left\{
\mathbf A\in\mathbb R^{2\times2}
\;\middle|\;
\mathbf A^\mathsf T=-\mathbf A
\right\}
=
\left\{
\alpha\mathbf S_2
\;\middle|\;
\alpha\in\mathbb R
\right\},
\]

where \(\alpha\) is a real scalar and

\[
\mathbf S_2
=
\begin{bmatrix}
0&-1\\
1&0
\end{bmatrix}.
\]

To justify the second equality, begin with a general matrix

\[
\mathbf A
=
\begin{bmatrix}
a&b\\
c&d
\end{bmatrix},
\qquad
a,b,c,d\in\mathbb R.
\]

The condition \(\mathbf A^\mathsf T=-\mathbf A\) requires corresponding
entries to satisfy

\[
a=-a,
\qquad
d=-d,
\qquad
c=-b.
\]

Therefore, \(a=d=0\). Setting \(\alpha=c=-b\) gives

\[
\mathbf A
=
\begin{bmatrix}
0&-\alpha\\
\alpha&0
\end{bmatrix}
=
\alpha\mathbf S_2.
\]

Thus, every \(2\times2\) skew-symmetric matrix is a scalar multiple of
\(\mathbf S_2\). Conversely, for every \(\alpha\in\mathbb R\),

\[
(\alpha\mathbf S_2)^\mathsf T
=
\alpha\mathbf S_2^\mathsf T
=
-\alpha\mathbf S_2,
\]

so every scalar multiple of \(\mathbf S_2\) is skew-symmetric. The two
sets are therefore equal. The notation \(\mathfrak{so}(2)\) is read
``little s o two.''

\subsubsection{Why these matrices form a tangent
space}\label{why-these-matrices-form-a-tangent-space}

The skew-symmetric matrix \(\mathbf S_2\) is tangent to \(SO(2)\) at the
identity rotation \(\mathbf I_2\). More generally, every scalar multiple
\(\alpha\mathbf S_2\), where \(\alpha\in\mathbb R\), is a possible
tangent direction there. This subsection explains the geometric meaning
of that statement and proves that these are all the possible tangent
directions.

A \textbf{tangent direction} to a curve at a point is the limiting
direction of secant lines drawn from that point to nearby points on the
curve. Equivalently, it is the derivative at that point of a
differentiable path that remains on the curve. This familiar definition
applies to \(SO(2)\) because every rotation matrix \(\mathbf R(\theta)\)
is determined by the pair \((\cos\theta,\sin\theta)\) on the unit
circle. Under this correspondence,

\[
\mathbf R(0)=\mathbf I_2
\quad\longleftrightarrow\quad
(\cos0,\sin0)=(1,0).
\]

For a nonzero angular increment \(h\) measured in radians, the secant
direction of the unit circle from \((1,0)\) to \((\cos h,\sin h)\) is

\[
\frac{1}{h}
\left(
\begin{bmatrix}
\cos h\\
\sin h
\end{bmatrix}
-
\begin{bmatrix}
1\\0
\end{bmatrix}
\right).
\]

As \(h\to0\), this secant direction approaches \([0\;1]^\mathsf T\), the
usual tangent direction to the circle at \((1,0)\). The corresponding
difference quotient in matrix coordinates is

\[
\frac{\mathbf R(h)-\mathbf I_2}{h}
=
\begin{bmatrix}
\dfrac{\cos h-1}{h}&-\dfrac{\sin h}{h}\\[6pt]
\dfrac{\sin h}{h}&\dfrac{\cos h-1}{h}
\end{bmatrix}.
\]

The scalar limits

\[
\lim_{h\to0}\frac{\sin h}{h}=1,
\qquad
\lim_{h\to0}\frac{\cos h-1}{h}=0
\]

give

\[
\boxed{
\left.
\frac{d\mathbf R(h)}{dh}
\right|_{h=0}
=
\lim_{h\to0}
\frac{\mathbf R(h)-\mathbf I_2}{h}
=
\mathbf S_2
}.
\]

The second scalar limit follows from \(\cos h-1=-2\sin^2(h/2)\), the
first limit, and the continuity of sine at zero. Therefore,
\(\mathbf S_2\) is tangent to the rotation curve at \(\mathbf I_2\) in
exactly the same sense that \([0\;1]^\mathsf T\) is tangent to the unit
circle at \((1,0)\).

To collect every possible tangent direction, let
\(\mathbf Q(s)\in SO(2)\) be any differentiable rotation curve defined
for the dimensionless real parameter \(s\) near zero, with
\(\mathbf Q(0)=\mathbf I_2\). The \textbf{tangent space of \(SO(2)\) at
\(\mathbf I_2\)} is

\[
T_{\mathbf I_2}SO(2)
\mathrel{:=}
\left\{
\left.
\frac{d\mathbf Q(s)}{ds}
\right|_{s=0}
\;\middle|\;
\mathbf Q(s)\in SO(2),\;
\mathbf Q(0)=\mathbf I_2
\right\}.
\]

The symbol \(T\) denotes a tangent space, and its subscript
\(\mathbf I_2\) identifies the point where the tangent directions are
attached. To prove that this space equals \(\mathfrak{so}(2)\), both set
inclusions must be established: every tangent direction must be shown to
be skew-symmetric, and every skew-symmetric matrix must be shown to
arise from a valid rotation curve.

First, take any \(\mathbf A\in T_{\mathbf I_2}SO(2)\). By definition,
\(\mathbf A=\mathbf Q'(0)\) for some rotation curve \(\mathbf Q(s)\)
through \(\mathbf I_2\). Differentiating
\(\mathbf Q(s)^\mathsf T\mathbf Q(s)=\mathbf I_2\) gives

\[
\mathbf Q'(s)^\mathsf T\mathbf Q(s)
+
\mathbf Q(s)^\mathsf T\mathbf Q'(s)
=
\mathbf 0_{2\times2}.
\]

Now evaluate at (s=0). Because the curve passes through the identity,

\[
\mathbf Q(0)=\mathbf I_2,
\qquad
\mathbf Q'(0)=\mathbf A.
\]

Substitution gives

\[
\mathbf A^\mathsf T+\mathbf A
=
\mathbf 0_{2\times2}.
\]

Thus, \(\mathbf A\) is skew-symmetric and belongs to
\(\mathfrak{so}(2)\). This proves

\[
T_{\mathbf I_2}SO(2)
\subseteq
\mathfrak{so}(2).
\]

Second, take any \(\mathbf A\in\mathfrak{so}(2)\). It has the form
\(\mathbf A=\alpha\mathbf S_2\) for some \(\alpha\in\mathbb R\). The
curve \(\mathbf Q(s)=\mathbf R(\alpha s)\) remains in \(SO(2)\), passes
through \(\mathbf I_2\) at \(s=0\), and satisfies

\[
\mathbf Q'(0)
=
\left.
\frac{d}{ds}\mathbf R(\alpha s)
\right|_{s=0}
=
\alpha\mathbf S_2
=
\mathbf A.
\]

Hence, every element of \(\mathfrak{so}(2)\) is produced by a valid
curve through the identity, which proves

\[
\mathfrak{so}(2)
\subseteq
T_{\mathbf I_2}SO(2).
\]

The two inclusions establish

\[
\boxed{
T_{\mathbf I_2}SO(2)
=
\mathfrak{so}(2)
=
\left\{
\alpha\mathbf S_2
\;\middle|\;
\alpha\in\mathbb R
\right\}
}.
\]

This proves that the skew-symmetric matrices in \(\mathfrak{so}(2)\) are
exactly the instantaneous directions in which a rotation curve can pass
through \(\mathbf I_2\). They describe rotational directions, not finite
rotations. The next subsection shows how these directions provide a
first-order approximation to nearby rotation matrices.

\subsubsection{Lie-group terminology and the local linear
model}\label{lie-group-terminology-and-the-local-linear-model}

Section~\ref{sec-se2-planar-rotations} established that \(SO(2)\) is a
group under matrix multiplication. Its matrices also vary smoothly with
the continuous angle \(\theta\), and its multiplication and inverse
operations are differentiable. A group with such a compatible smooth
structure is called a \textbf{Lie group}. The tangent space at the
identity of a Lie group, together with an operation called the Lie
bracket, is its \textbf{Lie algebra}. Thus, \(\mathfrak{so}(2)\) is the
Lie algebra of \(SO(2)\).

The Lie algebra is useful because it is linear even though the rotation
group is not. For any \(\alpha,\beta,c\in\mathbb R\),

\[
\alpha\mathbf S_2+\beta\mathbf S_2
=
(\alpha+\beta)\mathbf S_2,
\qquad
c(\alpha\mathbf S_2)
=
(c\alpha)\mathbf S_2,
\]

and both results remain in \(\mathfrak{so}(2)\). Instantaneous
rotational directions can therefore be added and scaled using ordinary
linear algebra, whereas adding or scaling finite rotation matrices does
not generally produce valid rotations.

The tangent space is also a local linear approximation of the group. For
a small angle \(h\),

\[
\boxed{
\mathbf R(h)
=
\mathbf I_2
+h\mathbf S_2
+O(h^2)
}.
\]

This result follows more directly from the first-order Taylor expansion
of the matrix-valued function \(\mathbf R(h)\) about \(h=0\):

\[
\mathbf R(h)
=
\mathbf R(0)
+h
\left.
\frac{d\mathbf R(h)}{dh}
\right|_{h=0}
+O(h^2).
\]
